\begin{document}

\maketitle

This file holds supporting material for the manuscript: the visibility audit of the
feature pipeline, the predictor families and the predictor scores on the two judging
traces and on the four non-judging ones (Section~S1); the field audit of the main
judging trace, what supports the shared-pool design, two checks on the
continuous-integration refit and the cost-concentration screen (Section~S2); the
experimental configuration, the selected budget points, the budget shapes the main guard
table no longer prints, the corrupted scores, the aging comparator, the alternative
ranking losses, the identity residual on all nine cells, the single-server configuration
and the trace-construction sensitivities (Section~S3); and the deferred proofs and
remarks of the theory section, the workload comparison and the consistency proposition
among them, including the proof of the net-overtake identity (Section~S4). Section,
table, figure, equation and statement numbers here carry an S prefix; a number without
it refers to the main manuscript, and the sentence says so. In the proofs, a job's
rank is its position in arrival order: a job of lower rank arrived earlier. The traces are the ones the manuscript's Table~\ref*{M-tab:traces} names: the
CodeBench judging logs \cite{codebench_dataset}, the Azure Functions invocation trace
\cite{azure2021trace}, the Intel Netbatch logs \cite{netbatch2012trace} and the two
Firefox continuous-integration pools collected from Mozilla's public APIs
\cite{taskcluster_docs,treeherder_docs}. The additional reproducibility specification is Section~S5; capacity, physical
queueing, feedback and LPC-EGEE results are Section~S6. Additional theory is Section~S7;
the extremal constructions and remaining proofs behind Section~\ref*{M-sec:theory} of the manuscript,
which carries no appendix of its own, are Section~S8; and the per-paper detail behind
Section~\ref*{M-sec:related} of the manuscript is Section~S9; the tables of the sealed run are
Section~S10; and the main comparisons replayed under the online protocol are Section~S11.
Outside Section~S10, every empirical figure quoted here is a development figure in
the sense of Section~\ref*{M-sec:exp_prov} of the manuscript: nothing in those sections comes
from a sealed part of any trace, and nothing there was recomputed for it.

%=================================================================
\section{Cost Prediction}
\label{sec:supp_pred}

\subsection{The Visibility Audit in Full}

Section~\ref*{M-sec:pred_visibility} of the manuscript states that every historical feature is recomputed by
streaming the log in availability order and reading each entity's state as of the query
instant, and that \devnum{0} rows disagree in any of the eight configurations. This is
what that test is.

On \devnum{3{,}000} randomly drawn development jobs all \devnum{34} history and
relational features are recomputed from scratch under each configuration's own
visibility rule and compared against the pipeline's values. The same test applied to the
natural but wrong construction, in which a feature may use any record with a smaller
index, fails on \devnum{97.67\%} of rows under the primary rule, so the test does
discriminate. The graph pipeline is checked separately: an index assertion over
\devnum{863{,}149} rows finds \devnum{0} timeline entries pointing at or after the read,
brute-force recomputation on \devnum{600} rows matches on all \devnum{2{,}400} root
states and all \devnum{2{,}400} neighbour sets, and perturbing or deleting every record
after a cut time leaves all earlier predictions unchanged while a positive control moves
the later ones.

\subsection{Predictors on the Non-Judging Traces}

Table~\ref{tab:s_pred_cross} is the cross-trace counterpart of
Table~\ref{tab:s_pred_edu}. The three figures Section~\ref*{M-sec:pred_comparison} of the manuscript quotes from it are the serverless STATIC
Spearman correlation and the two CI pools' ENT AUROC; the AUROC column of Table~\ref*{M-tab:cross} of
the manuscript carries the figure the scheduling results there are read against, which
is the ALL row of each trace.

A task on the first judging platform is attempted by one cohort within a few days, so
who is writing still carries information. A task on the second is attempted by
thousands of users over years, so the task's own record has already fixed the cost. The
non-judging traces push this further. On the serverless trace the identity of the
function explains \devnum{0.867} of the variance of $\log(1+C)$ on its own, and static
and calendar features alone are close to useless for ranking (Spearman
\devnum{0.021}). The two CI pools sit at opposite ends: entity history gives AUROC
\devnum{0.9968} on one and \devnum{0.6334} on the other, because that pool's job labels
do not separate its costs. The compute-farm trace is predictable in rank and not in
level.

\subsection{The Release Delay as a Sensitivity}
\label{sec:supp_lag}

The delay $d$ with which results reach the feature store (the rule of
Section~\ref*{M-sec:pred_visibility} of the manuscript) is treated as a sensitivity and not as
an assumption. At $d = 0$ the full tabular model reaches
AUROC \devnum{0.9283} and at $d = \devnum{600}$ s it reaches \devnum{0.9066}. On the second judging
platform, where the log carries no clock and lag has to be expressed in records, the same
model moves from \devnum{0.9266} at lag \devnum{0} to \devnum{0.8902} at lag
\devnum{1{,}000}. Ranking survives a ten-minute feedback delay at the top of the
curve; retrieval of the small heavy class degrades, average precision falling from
\devnum{0.289} to \devnum{0.139}.
% Source: evidence/codebench_service_v2/out_service_v2.txt, SUMMARY 1, table
% "auprc, all test rows", row (core, M4): column ires0 = 0.2891 and column
% ires600 = 0.1388.  The AUROC pair 0.9283 / 0.9066 of the sentence before it is the
% same row of the table "heavy-vs-not AUROC [95%% user-block CI], all test rows".
% The second platform: evidence/accoding_v2/out_accoding_v2.txt:395 (M4 AUROC 0.9266 at
% lag 0) and :567, :576 (stage boot --lag 1000, M4 AUROC 0.8902).


\subsection{Predictor Families}
\label{sec:supp_families}

Three model families are compared under one visibility protocol and one split: gradient-boosted
trees, a sequence model, and a relational ladder. The negative relational result of
Section~\ref*{M-sec:pred_comparison} of the manuscript is read off the last of them.


The tabular family is gradient-boosted trees~\cite{ke2017lightgbm} over five feature groups:
task history (M1), user history (M2), static features of the submitted artefact (M3),
everything tabular (M4), and M4 plus one-hop relational aggregates over the
task-assignment and user-cohort neighbourhoods (M5). A control (M6) permutes task
identity across assignments, so that M5's aggregates are keyed on a scrambled
neighbourhood; it reaches AUROC \devnum{0.9288} in the primary configuration against
M5's \devnum{0.9302}, so the aggregates lose almost nothing when the identity they are
built on is destroyed. The sequence family is a GRU over the earlier jobs of one
user whose results are already available, used alone (R1) and as a state vector appended
to M4's columns inside the boosted model (R1S). The relational family is a heterogeneous
network over user, task, assignment and cohort nodes~\cite{schlichtkrull2018rgcn} with no
node-identity embeddings, so an unseen task or user is handled by the same weights. Its
ablation ladder runs N0 (a multilayer perceptron on exactly M4's columns), G0U (the
relational architecture with every neighbour slot empty and the assignment and cohort
roots removed, which leaves exactly M4's information), G0 (empty neighbour slots, roots
kept), G1 (the real edges) and G2 (the same network on edges permuted within a period and
time block, so that neighbour counts and recency are preserved and only the identity of
the neighbour changes).

\subsection{The Cost of Predicting}
\label{sec:supp_predcost}

Prediction has to be cheap enough to sit in front of a queue whose median job is
\devnum{204} ms, which is what makes the choice between these predictors a queueing
decision and not a latency one. Measured in a streaming loop over \devnum{20{,}000} jobs, the
tabular path costs a median of \devnum{0.076} ms per job and a 99th percentile of
\devnum{0.188} ms; the relational network costs \devnum{0.434} ms per row plus
\devnum{0.121} ms of graph lookup, and the GRU \devnum{0.576} ms. Charging the measured
feature and inference latency to the predictive policies before they enqueue changes the
closed gap by less than \devnum{0.001} at every load we ran, so the choice between these
predictors is not a latency decision at this job size.


%=================================================================
\section{Traces and Workload Characterisation}
\label{sec:supp_data}

\subsection{The Field Audit of the Main Judging Trace}

Section~\ref*{M-sec:fields} of the manuscript states four readings of the main trace. This is the
evidence for them, and the two archive defects that follow from reading the trace this
way.

\paragraph{The timestamp is a result stamp.}
Among later-period runs the gap to the same user's previous event grows one for one with
the cost (slope \devnum{0.99} of the median gap on $C$) while the gap to the next event
barely moves (slope \devnum{0.16}), and the feedback after a user kills a run arrives
within about \devnum{0.1} s whatever its length. A result stamp predicts both readings; a
receipt stamp neither.

\paragraph{The whole-second leak.}
Stamps are recorded to the whole second while costs are recorded to the microsecond, so
the fractional part of a job's cost appears in the gap between its own stamp and the next
event, and a feature like ``seconds since the user's previous record'' reads the label off
the clock. We close it by replacing $t$ with $t + u$, where $u \in [0,1)$ is drawn
deterministically from a fixed key and the record's identifier.

\paragraph{The cost field and the limit.}
The cost field is wall-clock occupancy of one evaluation including interpreter start-up,
read from the 10th percentile of correct submissions (\devnum{0.12}--\devnum{0.18} s
across the two periods) and from its weak dependence on the number of test cases (median
\devnum{$0.196 + 0.007 n$} s in the later period). The platform limit is \devnum{60} s:
the last run to reach it ends at \devnum{2019-08-28} \devnum{10{:}32}, after which the
largest observed cost is \devnum{59.97} s, with \devnum{13} runs in $[59,60)$ s and none
above. Before that date there was no fixed limit, the longest run is \devnum{8{,}901} s,
and the \devnum{1{,}189} runs that end with a kill are almost all above \devnum{360.8} s,
pointing at a container watchdog rather than judging demand.

\paragraph{Co-ending groups, zero-cost blocks and two short archives.}
Groups of runs by the same user that end in the same second are \devnum{7.53\%} of blocks
and \devnum{26.28\%} of capped work; every prediction table in the manuscript is repeated
on rows outside such groups. A further \devnum{1{,}671} blocks record a cost of exactly
zero with empty outputs; they leave the simulated trace and stay in the features, the
labels and every total. The \devnum{2019-2} archive stops at \devnum{2019-09-19}, months
before that term's assessments end, and \devnum{2020-1} is short by about \devnum{3\%} of
its blocks; both serve per-submission modelling and are excluded from anything that treats
a term as a whole.

\paragraph{What the recorded costs mean.}
Part of the recorded duration is contention, which we then re-impose inside the pool:
between \devnum{38\%} and \devnum{79\%} of jobs longer than a second end in the same
second as another block of that student. The observed \devnum{60}-second regime is not
documentation of an enforced timeout, and how the platform enforces such a limit is
undocumented in the sources we found. We wrote to the maintainers about both points and
had no answer. ACcoding \cite{accoding2024} records no submission timestamps, so its
arrival process is synthetic.
% Twelfth pass: the paragraph above stood in Section 9 of the manuscript, which keeps
%   one sentence, the ACcoding citation and the pointers.

\paragraph{Against the published release statistics.}
We match the class count in \devnum{18} of \devnum{18} terms and the student and exercise
counts in \devnum{16} of \devnum{18}, and parse \devnum{4.08\%} fewer execution blocks
than the official column sums, whose printed totals disagree with those same columns by
\devnum{$-248$} exercises and \devnum{$+246{,}246$} blocks. We quote the columns.

\subsection{The Continuous-Integration Refit: Sharpness and Circularity}

Section~\ref*{M-sec:source_platform} of the manuscript reports the held-out refit of the effective capacity
$k_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and the setup term on the two Firefox CI pools. Two checks on that fit
sit here.

The objective is sharp in $k$ rather than flat: on a step-of-one grid only \devnum{5} to
\devnum{8} values of $k$ put the simulated mean wait within \devnum{10\%} of the recorded
one, so the capacity the fit chooses is not one of many equally good answers.

The circularity of the original whole-window fit can be read off the two pools directly.
On pool~A the whole-window and held-out rows are almost identical, because every split
picks nearly the same capacity; on pool~B the reverse split's held-out capacity gives a
mean ratio of \devnum{1.14} on its test part where the whole-window capacity gives
\devnum{0.99}. The difference between those two numbers is the size of the circularity
that fitting on the reported window would have hidden.

What the split does not cover is stated in Section~\ref*{M-sec:limits} of the manuscript and belongs here
in full. Out of sample the 90th percentile lands up to \devnum{21\%} from the recorded
one and is systematically low on the larger pool. The split is chronological inside one
\devnum{21}-day and one \devnum{7}-day window of two pools, not a different cluster,
quarter or workload, and the capacity is held constant over the test part although the
real machine count varies.

\subsection{What Supports the Shared-Pool Design}
\label{sec:supp_pool}

Section~\ref*{M-sec:source_platform} of the manuscript names three things in support of the shared-pool design
and gives each of them one sentence. The evidence behind all three is here.

Dedicated per-entity capacity is known to sit idle in the workloads this one resembles,
and two measurements outside our own traces point the same way. In the serverless
characterisation behind our invocation trace, \devnum{45\%} of applications are invoked
once an hour or less and \devnum{81\%} once a minute or less, and the authors report
that keeping them warm can cost far more than their billable execution
time~\cite{shahrad2020wild}. A cluster manager measured the opposite design directly:
splitting each large user's workload into its own cell would need \devnum{2} to
\devnum{16} times as many cells and \devnum{20} to \devnum{150\%} more
machines~\cite{verma2015borg}. Neither source is about judging, and neither licenses a
number of our own.
% Twelfth pass: the paragraph above stood in Section 7.3 of the manuscript, which keeps
%   one sentence and both citations.

The second is a measurement on the same demand. Replaying the overlay of
Section~\ref*{M-sec:overlay} of the manuscript under the dedicated design, the 99th percentile of containers
alive at once is \devnum{1{,}636} and each container executes code for \devnum{0.12\%} of
the time it is alive; since only \devnum{11\%} to \devnum{35\%} of logins have a logout
written after them, session boundaries are reconstructed, and across six reconstructions
that percentile lies between \devnum{1{,}003} and \devnum{2{,}525} containers and the
utilisation between \devnum{0.07\%} and \devnum{0.17\%}. A shared pool holds the 99th
percentile of deadline-window waits under \devnum{1} s with \devnum{14} servers under
FCFS and \devnum{13} under the predictor-ordered policy. Pooling is worth two orders of
magnitude here and the scheduler is worth one server (\devnum{14} to \devnum{13} at
\devnum{1} s, \devnum{11} to \devnum{10} at \devnum{5} s, \devnum{8} to \devnum{7} at
\devnum{30} s). It does not support a claim that the platform ever queued, the pooled
side being simulated, nor that the containers are over-provisioned, since they also run
the editing session the pool does not replace, nor that any of it is a cost saving.
% Source: evidence/consolidation/out_SUMMARY.txt: 1,636 and 0.12% at :76 (proxy1800) and
% :225; 1,003 and 2,525 at :73-74 and :106; 0.07% to 0.17% at :106; 11% to 35% of logins
% with a logout at :200-201; server counts 14/13, 11/10, 8/7 at :135, :227 and :246.

The third is that the simulator reproduces waits somebody else recorded. The CI logs carry
the queue wait of every run, so we replay the recorded arrivals and services through our
own $k$-server non-preemptive kernel. Two quantities are fitted: the effective capacity
$k_{\mathrm{eff}}$, the shortfall against the listed machine count being machines that are
up but not claiming work, and a setup term, the median short gap between consecutive runs
on the same machine, added to every service because a machine is not reusable the instant
a run resolves. Fitting both on the window and then reporting agreement on that same
window would be circular, so we refit both on one chronological part of each window and
test on the other, five splits over the two pools.
This split is internal to the recorded window and is held out of the fit only; it
is not sealed in the sense of Section~\ref*{M-sec:sealed} of the manuscript, and the whole window has
been read.

The capacity is stable across splits. On pool~A the three splits choose \devnum{155},
\devnum{155} and \devnum{157} of \devnum{173} listed machines, and on pool~B the two
splits choose \devnum{96} and \devnum{92} of \devnum{105}; the setup term moves between
\devnum{54.2} and \devnum{55.3} s on the first and between \devnum{126.2} and
\devnum{128.6} s on the second. The objective is sharp in $k$. With those parameters
frozen, the held-out part comes
back at \devnum{0.90} to \devnum{1.14} times the recorded mean wait, \devnum{0.86} to
\devnum{1.16} at the 99th percentile and \devnum{0.89} to \devnum{1.28} at the 99.9th,
with a per-hour mean-wait correlation of \devnum{0.936} to \devnum{0.981}, a per-job rank
correlation of \devnum{0.940} to \devnum{0.963}, and a median per-job absolute error of
\devnum{16} to \devnum{84} s.

The 90th percentile does not survive the held-out test. It lands at \devnum{0.81} to
\devnum{1.21}
of the recorded one, and on the larger pool it is below the recorded value on all three
splits while the 99th is above it, so the simulated distribution is too steep in its upper
middle; the two-term objective hides this in sample by trading the mean against the p90,
so we fit out of sample and say so. What the fit absorbs is nonetheless a
property of the pool and no artefact of fitting: told instead that every listed
machine is available, the same simulator underestimates the held-out mean wait by a factor
of \devnum{1.3} to \devnum{2.6} and its per-hour correlation falls to \devnum{0.75}.
Section~S2.2 gives the step-of-one sharpness check and reads the circularity
of the original whole-window fit off the two pools directly.
% Sources of the three paragraphs above:
%   evidence/firefox_ci_holdout/out_SUMMARY.txt, section A (k_eff step-1 per split and the
%   setup term on the fit part: osx 155/155/157 of 173, talos 96/92 of 105; setup 54.2-55.3 s
%   and 126.2-128.6 s), section B (the HELD OUT rows: mean x, p99 x, p99.9 x, rho_h, sp,
%   med|e|, and the "no fit k = nominal machines" rows for the 1.3-2.6x and the 0.75),
%   section E (5-8 values of k inside 10% of the recorded mean) and section F (the p90
%   range 0.81-1.21 and the circularity comparison 1.14 vs 0.99 on the talos reverse split).
%   The same rows are in holdout.csv.  Pool A = releng-hardware/gecko-t-osx-1500-m4
%   (173 machines, 21 d), pool B = releng-hardware/gecko-t-linux-talos-2404 (105, 7 d),
%   matching Table~2 of the manuscript.  The sharpness check and the circularity read-off
%   moved to supplementary.tex, Section~S2.2 (referee item G9).

\label{sec:supp_tails}

The cost-concentration screen is read trace by trace below, with the arrival and service
fields each trace supplies and what each analysis truncation removes. Section~\ref*{M-sec:load} of the
manuscript quotes its headline shares.

\label{tab:s_tails}

\hline

% Sources of Table~\ref{tab:s_tails}:
%   CodeBench rows: evidence/codebench_audit/out_tail_and_counts.txt (eras 2020-ERE..2022-2
%     and 2018-1..2019-2, columns top1_share / top5_share, raw C and C_cap = min(C,60)).
%   ACcoding rows and both per-job limits: evidence/accoding_v2/out_accoding_v2.txt,
%     stage compare section B (time_cost and 0.5 + time_cost columns).
%   Serverless and compute farm: evidence/cross_domain/out_cross_domain_table.txt section A
%     (L used column) and out_audit_azure.txt / out_audit_netbatch.txt (work-share lines).
%   CI pools: evidence/firefox_ci/out_SUMMARY.txt section A ("share of total work carried
%     by the top" and "L' (train p99.9 of service)").

\paragraph{Arrival and service provenance across traces.}
CodeBench's jittered result stamp minus the recorded duration is an execution-start
proxy, not a measured receipt time. ACcoding has no submission clock: arrivals are
drawn, including the Poisson comparison and the deadline-shaped construction; its
submission ids determine ordering, not elapsed time. Over \devnum{469} contests, the
Spearman correlation between a contest's first submission id and its recorded start time
is \devnum{0.9995}, but in \devnum{207} of \devnum{468} consecutive transitions the id
ranges of two contests overlap.
% Source: evidence/accoding_v2/out_accoding_v2.txt:170-171. Moved from Section 7 of the
%   manuscript in the revision of 2026-09-24. Its measured cost sums test
case execution times, each subject to its problem limit, and the replay adds an
assumed \devnum{0.5} s overhead. Thus neither the observed \devnum{30.0} s sum nor
the resulting \devnum{30.5} s maximum is a verified whole-job timeout.
% Source: evidence/accoding_v2/out_accoding_v2.txt, field audit and compare section B.

For Azure, the replay arrival is the documented end timestamp minus duration,
again a start proxy; the publisher explicitly states that invocation timestamps
were modified from the production trace \cite{azure2021trace}. The Netbatch replay
uses the recorded SWF submission time and run duration. Their bounds in
Table~\ref{tab:s_tails} are training p99.9 truncations, not observed platform timeouts.
Both FCFS and every reordered policy receive the same truncated service sequence.
The truncation affects \devnum{1{,}187} Azure jobs and \devnum{7{,}696} Netbatch jobs
in the analysed pools; it removes \devnum{3.18\%} and \devnum{2.16\%} of raw work.
% Sources: evidence/cross_domain/meta_azure.csv and meta_netbatch.csv;
% evidence/cross_domain/out_leaktest_azure.txt and out_leaktest_netbatch.txt,
% clipped counts; out_audit_azure.txt and out_audit_netbatch.txt, work removed.

In the CI pools, arrival is the recorded scheduled instant, when dependencies have
cleared and a worker may claim the run; recorded wait is started minus scheduled.
Service is resolved minus started plus a fitted same-worker setup term. The analysis
first adds setup and then clips this occupancy at the training p99.9 $L$;
longer jobs are retained with truncated service, not excluded. This clips
\devnum{227} runs in pool~A and \devnum{27} in pool~B. FCFS and all comparison
policies use exactly that same capped occupancy sequence. The source scheduler's
priority-then-FIFO rule and operational capacity are distinct from the counterfactual
FCFS model used for the guarantee.
% Sources: evidence/firefox_ci/out_leaktest_releng-hardware__gecko-t-osx-1500-m4.txt and
% out_leaktest_releng-hardware__gecko-t-linux-talos-2404.txt;
% evidence/firefox_ci/meta_releng-hardware__gecko-t-osx-1500-m4.csv and
% meta_releng-hardware__gecko-t-linux-talos-2404.csv; fci_build.py and fci_sim.py.

An enforced $L$ in a deployed service would instead kill an unfinished job at the
limit and charge that executed work to the same-input FCFS comparison. The killed
job would not yield its intended result; these replays do not estimate the resulting
completion-quality cost or users' resubmission response. The table therefore
supports finite-input bounds under its stated transformations, not a future
operational timeout claim.


\subsection{The Steady-State Formula against This Load}
\label{sec:supp_steady}

Section~\ref*{M-sec:model} of the manuscript reports that the non-preemptive priority formula of
\cite{harcholbalter2013} is out by an order of magnitude on this load. This is the
comparison behind the two figures it quotes.

The synthetic control is a Poisson stream whose service distribution matches the first
two moments of the real one. On it the formula agrees with simulation to
\devnum{0.4\%}, so the implementation of the formula is not what fails. On the same
workload replayed at its recorded timestamps the measured wait is
\devnum{10.7}--\devnum{13.7} times what the formula predicts on the development trace.
A class threshold tuned on the formula would therefore be tuned against a number that
is off by an order of magnitude, which is why the manuscript states every promise on
the realised sample path instead.
% Thirteenth pass: the measurement above stood in Section 3 of the manuscript, which
%   keeps the two figures and this pointer.

\subsection{Deliberately Corrupted Scores}
\label{sec:supp_adv}

Under a reversed score, a random score and a longest-called-shortest score, the guard
still holds every job inside its promise, and the deadline-window percentile is not
rescued. Table~\ref{tab:s_adv} is the measurement Section~\ref*{M-sec:exp_guard} of the manuscript reads.

\subsection{The Residual on All Nine Cells}

Table~\ref{tab:s_resid} gives the residual $\Delta_i$ of
Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:identity} of the manuscript on all nine cells of overlay~0, and
Section~\ref*{M-sec:exp_bound} of the manuscript quotes its largest ratio. Read across the
cells, the residual is a property of the schedule and barely moves with the policy: inside
each load the ratios are similar in scale, but are not equal to the third decimal.
An $R^2$ rounded to unity is likewise not exact equality.

\subsection{A Single Server}

At $k = 1$ the additive constant $(3 - 2/k)L$ is exactly $L$, and the identity of
Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:identity} of the manuscript holds with no error term.
Table~\ref{tab:s_k1} gives the configuration in full. Guard($\devnum{600}$) and
Fixed($\devnum{600}$) close \devnum{0.854} and \devnum{0.868} of the gap, with overlapping
intervals, but differ by a factor of \devnum{3.41} in harm; the same comparison gives
\devnum{3.01} at $G = \devnum{300}$ s and \devnum{12.18} at $G = \devnum{1200}$ s, so the
looser the promise, the more the shape of the budget matters. The realised excess reaches
\devnum{595.5} s against the promised \devnum{600} s, and \devnum{597.0} s under the worst
corrupted score: at one server almost all of the promise is used. The parameters are the
ones the selection rule chose on the \devnum{15} multiserver validation cells; $k = 1$ is
not among them, the jointly selected shape transfers at the two tighter promises and not
at $G = \devnum{1200}$ s (Section~\ref{sec:supp_k1shape}), and we do not re-tune on it.
% Source: the rows of Table~\ref{tab:s_k1} (outputs/dev_tables/k1/main_table.csv,
%   Guard(G) and Fixed(G): gap_closed, harm_s, max_excess_s; the harm factors are the
%   Fixed/Guard ratios of harm_s at each G). Moved from Section 8 of the manuscript in the
%   revision of 2026-09-24.

The firing rate of the three unguarded policies is printed \verb|---|: the column counts
dispatch epochs at which the guard overrides the base policy, and these three runs have no
guard. Scoring first-come first-served at \devnum{100\%} by the convention that every
first-come first-served dispatch serves the queue head, as an earlier version of this
table did, compares it with the guarded runs on a quantity it does not have.

\subsection{Outcome Visibility: Exposure, Lag and the Variants}
\label{sec:supp_visibility}

Four things stand behind the headline of Section~\ref*{M-sec:exp_visibility} of the manuscript: the online
simulation that produces it, the same-copy queueing exposure that the online and exact
protocols correct for, an audit of the release lag of the conservative reference, and the
references its table names. All four are defined here.

The conservative figure can be taken apart. At the busiest load, Guard($\devnum{600}$)
closes \devnum{0.429} of the gap under the conservative reference and unguarded SPJF-E
\devnum{0.670}. Applying the reference's three restrictions to the frozen original model
gives \devnum{0.536}, and refitting the models under them accounts for the rest of the fall
to \devnum{0.429}: of the fall from the original \devnum{0.801}, the restrictions account
for \devnum{0.265} and the refit for \devnum{0.107}. Leaving out, in addition, the results
of other classes in the same term moves the exact figure from \devnum{0.672} to
\devnum{0.655}.
% Sources: outputs/dev_consistent_visibility/exact_comparison.csv, level=2,
% SPJF-E|conservative, gap_closed; attribution_comparison.csv, level=2,
% Guard(600)|combined_frozen and Guard(600)|conservative, gap_closed;
% exact_sensitivity_comparison.csv, level=2, Guard(600)|exact_other_class_withheld,
% gap_closed. Moved from Section 8 of the manuscript in the revision of 2026-09-24.

Let $H_i$ be the set of distinct outcome records used by score $i$, counting a record
only once if it enters several entity aggregates. Separate records from the same
class-term copy, histories from other classes or terms, and records with no
submission job in the replayed pool. Same-copy queueing exposure is
\[
 V_i(P)=\{j\in H_i:\ \operatorname{copy}(j)=\operatorname{copy}(i),\quad
             a_j+W_P[j]+C_j>a_i\}.
\]
An external past record is not unavailable merely because it has no replayed job.
The earlier audit mapped histories through the same replication round while
different classes were independently shifted. Its roughly \devnum{80\%} affected
jobs and \devnum{95.39\%} with unreplayed history are mapping-exposure statistics,
not estimates of same-copy queueing leakage. Section~S5 specifies
the external-history clock and the separate class-history sensitivity.
% Source: outputs/dev_visibility/visibility_exposure.csv, overall_affected_share
% (approximately 0.80 across cells), unreplayed_overall_affected_share=0.9538935602.

The conservative score restricts user, exercise and pair history to submission
outcomes in the same class-term copy, and admits a result only when
$\mathrm{done}_j+\devnum{3600}\le a_i$, with times in seconds. The static score
uses code, course and clock information known at submission, without outcome
history. Both use their own fitted models; original, conservative and static
therefore differ in more than release delay. Their differences cannot be attributed
to queueing delay alone and are not mathematical upper or lower performance bounds.
Guard parameters remain those selected under the original score. The online protocol
below is the empirical headline and the exact protocol its stricter reference; the
original-score results are optimistic references, and the conservative and static
results are controls.
% Source: outputs/dev_visibility/manifest.json, notes.conservative_lag_s and
% selected_parameters_unchanged; headline: configs/main.yaml, features.headline_variant.

For a same-copy submission, $W_P[j]\le D$ is a sufficient availability condition
under a source release lag $D$. The largest development FCFS wait is
\devnum{1544.602708} s. Adding the largest evaluated promise, \devnum{1200} s,
still lies below the \devnum{3600} s lag. All conservative Guard and FCFS cells
have \devnum{0} lag violations. Unguarded conservative SPJF-E has
\devnum{336} job occurrences with waits beyond the lag across the evaluated cells;
it lacks the guard's certificate, so its performance is a lagged-score comparator
with residual availability violations. A new pool must pass the per-job lag audit;
the development maximum does not certify it in advance.
% Source: outputs/dev_visibility/visibility_waits_and_lag.csv, max(wait_max_s)
% for FCFS; sum(lag_violations) for conservative SPJF-E=336 and for FCFS/Guard=0;
% outputs/dev_visibility/manifest.json, promises and conservative_lag_s.

The online protocol freezes the original model weights and simulates each policy once.
When job $i$ arrives, its score is computed from the history a deployed scheduler would
hold at $a_i$: every record from another class or term at its recorded time, and a
same-copy record $j$ only if $a_j+W_P[j]+C_j\le a_i$ in this simulation, that is, only if
job $j$ has already completed. Rolling windows over the same copy are ordered by these
completion times. A score depends only on completions before its job arrives, and those
depend only on the scores of jobs that arrived earlier, so one pass defines the
simulation. We implement it by pausing the simulator at every arrival whose visible
same-copy history differs from the one its current score was computed on, recomputing
that job's history and score, and resuming. A second implementation reaches the same
simulation by iteration: simulate, rescore every job whose visible history differs, and
simulate again until none does. On the first overlay at the lightest load the two agree
job for job for SPJF-E, Guard($\devnum{600}$) and Timeout($\devnum{600}$), in every wait
and in every rescored score. About \devnum{255{,}000} of the \devnum{17.6} million arrivals
pause, and the pausing implementation needs \devnum{4} to \devnum{5} minutes per policy
against \devnum{15} to \devnum{26} for the iteration. In every development cell,
\devnum{2{,}000} jobs drawn at random and up to \devnum{2{,}000} rescored ones are scored
again directly from their visible history, and every one matches the simulation.
% Sources: outputs/online_probe/.checkpoints/o0_l0/*.json (metric rows, audit, elapsed_s,
% pass_rows: pauses 254,788 / 256,499 / 257,271; online 234 / 268 / 304 s; iteration
% 1,033 / 1,582 / 895 s) and deltas/*.npz (identical job_index and corrected_score);
% outputs/dev_online_visibility/online_audit.csv, mismatches = 0 in every row. ADR 0008.

The exact protocol freezes the original model weights, replays each policy, leaves out
same-copy outcomes still unavailable at the target arrival, recomputes affected
histories and scores, and repeats until no used outcome violates availability. The set
of left-out outcomes only grows, so this finite procedure terminates. Every development cell,
five overlays by three loads by five policies, and every sensitivity cell ended its
final pass with no used outcome unavailable. The procedure certifies availability in
that final replay. Unlike the online protocol, it never returns a left-out result, even
one its final replay completes in time, so it can use less history than a deployed
scheduler would hold. The visibility table of Section~\ref*{M-sec:exp_visibility}
of the manuscript prints Guard(600) under all five protocols. The other policies under
each protocol, the same-copy exposure statistics of each replay, and the attribution
ladder that separates leaving results out from refitting are in the package output directory
\texttt{outputs/dev\_consistent\_visibility/}, in \texttt{exact\_comparison.csv},
\texttt{same\_copy\_exposure.csv} and \texttt{attribution\_comparison.csv}; the
emitted tables \texttt{tab\_exact\_visibility.tex} and
\texttt{tab\_same\_copy\_exposure.tex} under \texttt{outputs/consistent\_paper\_tables/}
typeset the first two.

\label{sec:supp_aging}

A linear aging credit, selected under the conservative score and the guard's harm limit,
carries no proved per-job guarantee and closes far less of the gap.
Section~\ref*{M-sec:exp_guard} of the manuscript states the comparison; here it is run.

It uses the conservative score of Section~\ref{sec:supp_visibility}, with a validation search

the reference promise used to set the validation constraint, and carries no proved
per-job guarantee. All evaluated loads and harmful outcomes are retained.

\subsection{Package Predictor Metrics and Their Splits}
\label{sec:supp_devpred}

The AUROC of Table~\ref*{M-tab:predictors} of the manuscript and the AUROC of the deployed
expected-cost score are not the same measurement. The first belongs to the retrospective
classifier comparison, the second to the score the scheduler uses. This is what separates
them.

The AUROC \devnum{0.928} in the earlier predictor comparison is M4 under the
source-clock I-res protocol, using heavy-class classifier probabilities averaged
over seeds, trained on the core terms and evaluated on
\devnum{2022-2}. It is not the AUROC of the package's deployed expected-cost score.
The latter is \devnum{0.9360} pooled over rolling forward development predictions
and \devnum{0.9372} on \devnum{2022-2}; the conservative pooled score is
\devnum{0.9116}. Table~\ref{tab:s_development_predictor} uses the package values and
split definitions that will accompany the sealed predictor column. The earlier
value remains evidence for its own retrospective classifier comparison, whose
predictions never enter scheduling; it is not a competing estimate of the same fitted score.

\subsection{Alternative Ranking Losses}
\label{sec:supp_scores}

Alternative ranking losses were fitted on the same features and run through the same
simulator (Table~\ref{tab:s_scores}). The fitted quantile scores and raw-second squared-error model lose to the Tweedie
fit at every load in this comparison. Raw squared error and the Tweedie mean loss
have the same unrestricted population target, so this does not identify a change
of estimand as the cause. Finite model capacity, error weighting, regularisation
and optimisation can produce different rankings for the same target. A two-class rule that
sends predicted-heavy jobs behind predicted-light ones and runs FCFS inside each class reaches a
deadline-window p99 of \devnum{64.05} s at $\rho = \devnum{0.8}$ on the first overlay, against
\devnum{43.86} s for the full ordering on the same overlay, so most of the benefit comes from
the fine ordering inside the light class and little of it from separating the two classes.

\subsection{What the Shape of the Budget Is Worth}
\label{sec:supp_shape}

The two shapes the main table no longer prints are read here through the paired differences and
in full in Table~\ref{tab:s_guard_abl}, all three at one fixed promise. The
shape matters where the promise is tight and the load is high, and hardly at all elsewhere. At
$G = \devnum{600}$ s the constant shape trails the joint winner by
\devnum{0.007 [0.003, 0.012]}, \devnum{0.028 [0.020, 0.038]} and \devnum{0.119 [0.074, 0.176]}
of the gap as the load rises through the three levels, each interval clear of zero; at
$G = \devnum{300}$ s the same three differences are \devnum{0.008 [-0.002, 0.015]},
\devnum{0.099 [0.056, 0.157]} and \devnum{0.262 [0.202, 0.288]}; and at $G = \devnum{1200}$ s
they are \devnum{0.003 [-0.000, 0.008]}, \devnum{0.005 [-0.001, 0.011]} and
\devnum{0.017 [0.002, 0.031]}; only the last, at the busiest load, clears zero, and barely. At
a loose promise and a light load the shape is not doing measurable work.

The other axis is harm. At $G = \devnum{600}$ s the queue-length shape reaches within
\devnum{0.009} of the joint winner's gap at every load while its worst-overlay harm is
\devnum{106.0}, \devnum{117.7} and \devnum{123.4} s against the winner's \devnum{237.0},
\devnum{319.6} and \devnum{339.9} s, under half at every load. That is also why
the rule picks the queue-length shape at $G = \devnum{1200}$ s, where the three shapes are
within \devnum{0.005} of one another on the validation objective and it has the smallest worst
harm. The shape decides which jobs pay for the promise more than it decides how much of the
benefit survives.

Fixed($G$), which spends the whole cap, is an equal-promise candidate in the search,
but is infeasible under the validation harm constraint at every promise.
Its evaluation-overlay harm is \devnum{181.8}, \devnum{195.4} and \devnum{208.2} s at
$G = \devnum{300}$ s against a \devnum{150} s limit, \devnum{237.0}, \devnum{447.1} and
\devnum{465.5} s at $G = \devnum{600}$ s against \devnum{300} s, and \devnum{237.0},
\devnum{469.2} and \devnum{708.8} s at $G = \devnum{1200}$ s against \devnum{600} s.
Thus the evaluation cells also exceed the corresponding limits at each promise.
It is ahead of the joint winner on the gap
closed in eight of the nine cells, by up to \devnum{0.028}, so it buys its extra gap with harm
above the declared limit in some cells; we make no claim that any shape beats it on
the gap at equal promise.

% worst_harm_s; outputs/dev_tables/main_table.csv, Fixed(G), harm_s and gap_closed. The
% limits 150/300/600 s are G/2: outputs/selection_v3/selected_parameters.csv harm_limit_s,
% configs/main.yaml harm_fraction 0.5.

\subsection{The Identity, Measured Job by Job}
\label{sec:supp_identity}

Section~\ref*{M-sec:theory} of the manuscript states that neither exhaustive search nor real input reaches
the error term of Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:identity}. Exhaustive and random search over millions of small
instances never exceeds $(k-1)L$ by more than one time unit, and on the
\devnum{17.6}-million-job trace of Section~\ref*{M-sec:experiments} of the manuscript the largest residual
reaches \devnum{0.470} to \devnum{0.518} of $2(k-1)L$, again about $(k-1)L$. That is
where a search which does not build the cascade of Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:tight1} of the manuscript stops,
so the measurement says the workload does not attain the constant and not that the
constant is loose. The constant is where Section~\ref*{M-sec:theory} of the manuscript put it: the
family that approaches it is a geometric cascade needing hundreds of jobs arranged
against the wrapper, and nothing in a replayed workload arranges them.
% PROVENANCE (lines above).  The range 0.470-0.518 is the minimum and the maximum
% of column ratio_to_bound over all 81 rows of
% outputs/dev_tables/identity_residuals.csv: min 0.46982235 (overlay 0, level 0,
% k = 8, Skip(300)), max 0.51727123 (overlay 0, level 1, k = 5, Guard(300)).
% Printed rounded outwards, so that the same 0.518 serves the "never exceeds"
% reading in Section 8 of the manuscript; the stale 0.485/0.521 pair came from
% an earlier run and is not reproducible from the released package.
% Ninth pass: this paragraph stood in Section 6.4 of the manuscript.

The net-overtake identity had been tested on small synthetic instances and never measured on
the trace this paper runs on, so we measured it. Table~\ref{tab:s_resid} reports the residual
$\Delta_i = k(W_P[i] - W_{\mathrm{FCFS}}[i]) - (\mathrm{In}_i - \mathrm{Out}_i)$ on overlay~0
at the three load levels under Guard($\devnum{600}$), SPJF-E and the reversed score,
with FCFS on the same input as the reference schedule in every cell. Recovering $\mathrm{In}_i$ and
$\mathrm{Out}_i$ needs the dispatch sequence, which the guard kernel does not expose, so the
kernel and the independent reference simulator were each copied verbatim and given recorders
that add no branch and no state update; the two agree on the dispatch sequence of every job of
\devnum{3{,}000} random instances, the recording kernel reproduces the production kernel's
waits element for element inside every cell, and $\mathrm{In}_i$ and $\mathrm{Out}_i$ are
carried in exact integer microseconds, the currency the guard itself meters.

Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:identity} of the manuscript is asserted on every job of every cell: \devnum{0} violations over
\devnum{1{,}428{,}415{,}560} job-checks, the \devnum{27} policies of this overlay at the three
loads. The residual stays far from its ceiling. The
largest $|\Delta_i|$ over the nine cells of Table~\ref{tab:s_resid} is \devnum{6.788}$L$, at
$k = \devnum{8}$, where the
bound $2(k-1)L$ is \devnum{14}$L$, and over those nine cells the maximum sits between
\devnum{0.485} and \devnum{0.514} of the bound, which is about $(k-1)L$; over all
\devnum{81} cells the largest is \devnum{0.5173} of the bound, so it never exceeds
\devnum{0.518}, for the reason Section~\ref{sec:supp_guardmeas} gives. Same-phase bookkeeping, the mechanism
behind the degenerate extremal instances of Remark~\ref{rem:directions} above, accounts for at most
\devnum{0.36\%} of the total $\mathrm{In}$ in any of those nine cells, so the executed-work
convention would not change the picture for these
policies. It is not negligible everywhere: under the randomised ranking, which ties many jobs
into one dispatch instant, the share reaches \devnum{14.95\%}.
$(\mathrm{In}_i - \mathrm{Out}_i)/k$ explains $R^2 = \devnum{0.983}$ to \devnum{0.9996} of the
variance of $W_P[i] - W_{\mathrm{FCFS}}[i]$, with a worst per-job error of \devnum{44.9} to
\devnum{50.9} s where the theorem permits $(2-2/k)L$, that is \devnum{90} s at $k = 4$ and
\devnum{105} s at $k = 8$. This is one overlay of development data, and the residual is a
property of a schedule rather than a statistic, so no interval is attached to it.

\input{figures/fig_excess}

\subsection{Choosing a Shape on One Set of Cells}
\label{sec:supp_k1shape}

The single-server configuration also shows the cost of choosing a shape on one set of cells.
The parameters were selected on the \devnum{15} multiserver validation cells, and $k = 1$ is
not among them. At $G = \devnum{300}$ and $\devnum{600}$ s the joint winner transfers: it closes
\devnum{0.746} and \devnum{0.854} of the gap, against \devnum{0.543} and \devnum{0.791} for the
constant shape. At $G = \devnum{1200}$ s it does not: the queue-length shape that wins the
joint comparison on the multiserver cells closes \devnum{0.748} here, while the age-relative
shape closes \devnum{0.895} and the constant shape \devnum{0.865}. Section~\ref*{M-sec:exp_k1} of the
manuscript says what we do with that and Section~\ref*{M-sec:limits} there says what it means.

The promise itself is unaffected, since it holds for every budget under the cap; what
the shape decides is how much of the base policy's benefit survives. We report the
number the pre-stated rule produces and do not re-tune on the configuration that
exposes it. An operator whose pool is much smaller than the validation pool should
re-run the selection rather than take our shape. The three shapes also differ in where
the harm falls, so which is preferable depends on a judgement about the jobs first-come
first-served would have started at once, and that judgement is the operator's.

\subsection{Trace-Construction Sensitivities}
\label{sec:supp_sens}

Five trace-construction variants were run through an earlier source-clock pipeline
(Table~\ref{tab:s_sens}). These are sensitivity results for its stored M4 log-scale
score and fixed completed-work budget, not the selected expected-cost Guard($G$).
Within that earlier configuration, the alternatives retain a positive gap; adding the term whose busiest
hour is dominated by runs that outlived their container raises the gap, and the opposite reading
of the timestamp, in which the stamp is receipt rather than completion, gives
\devnum{0.926} against \devnum{0.895}. Merging or dropping the groups of runs by one user that
end in the same second moves the gap closed by \devnum{0.017} and \devnum{0.003} at full load,
so that earlier result survives these two treatments of co-ending runs;
delaying the visibility of a completed job's outcome costs prediction quality and not much of
it (Section~\ref*{M-sec:prediction} of the manuscript). The earlier fixed-budget guard is more sensitive to the
construction, ranging from \devnum{0.442} to \devnum{0.843} of the gap at a fixed budget of
\devnum{600} s at full load. What these variants show is that the earlier ordering
experiment is sensitive to how the timestamps are read, to which source terms are
included, and to how co-ending runs are treated. They validate neither the final
selected Guard($G$), nor conservative history, nor the policy-specific exact protocol.
A trace built from the last development term alone is not available this way: loading a
pool of four from it would take \devnum{190} copies of its \devnum{8} class-terms, which
stacks every deadline of one term on the same weekday and hour.

The worst wait among heavy jobs, over the five overlays, is \devnum{487},

\devnum{3{,}313} and \devnum{6{,}286} s under SPJF-E, and \devnum{921}, \devnum{1{,}467}
and \devnum{1{,}993} s under Guard($\devnum{600}$). The guard therefore leaves the worst
heavy job within \devnum{434}, \devnum{444} and \devnum{450} s of the FCFS figure, inside
the promise, where the unguarded ranking multiplies it by \devnum{2.8}, \devnum{3.2} and
\devnum{4.1}.

\section{Deferred Proofs}
\label{sec:supp_proofs}

Section~\ref*{M-sec:theory} of the manuscript gives the ideas behind the arguments proved here. The
workload comparison uses continuity and left limits, whereas consistency is proved by
induction; the strictness at $k \ge 2$ runs the same way as the bound itself, in that at
the first instant $t_1$ at which $D = U_{Q_1} - U_{Q_2}$ touches $(k-1)L$ the linear
piece to its left must have positive slope, which forces at most $k-1$ jobs present
under $Q_1$ there, so none of them waits, and left limits then make $U_{Q_1}$ strictly
decreasing on that piece, so it cannot equal its own supremum at $t_1$. The consistency
induction needs two further readings, both one line: its first consequence uses
$\mathrm{over}[q](t) \le \mathrm{In}^A_q$, a completed overtaker of $q$ having been
dispatched while $q$ waited, and the second uses Corollary~\ref*{M-cor:necessary} of the manuscript. The
tightness constructions and the remaining proofs are
Section~\ref{sec:supp_constructions} of this file. We use the manuscript's notation:
$U_Q(t)$ is the unfinished work at time $t$ under policy $Q$, and $N_Q(t)$ is the
number of jobs present.

\subsection{The Workload Comparison}
\label{sec:supp_gap}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma~\ref*{M-lem:gap} of the manuscript]
Put $D = U_{Q_1} - U_{Q_2}$. Arrivals add the same jump to both terms, so $D$ is
continuous, and between arrivals it changes at rate
$-(\min(N_{Q_1},k) - \min(N_{Q_2},k))$; it is therefore piecewise linear with
breakpoints among the finitely many arrival and completion instants of the two
runs.

If $U_{Q_1}(u) > (k-1)L$ then more than $k-1$ jobs are present under $Q_1$, each
carrying at most $L$, so every server of $Q_1$ is busy and
$\min(N_{Q_1},k) = k \ge \min(N_{Q_2},k)$: $D$ does not increase at $u$. Fix $t$
and let
$S = \{u \le t : U_{Q_1}(u) \le (k-1)L\}$, which is non-empty because $U_{Q_1}$
vanishes
before the first arrival, and put $t_0 = \sup S$. The supremum need not be
attained, since $U_{Q_1}$ jumps upwards at arrivals, so we use the left limit:
there are $u_n \uparrow t_0$ with $U_{Q_1}(u_n) \le (k-1)L$, hence
$U_{Q_1}(t_0^-) \le (k-1)L$ and, $D$ being continuous,
$D(t_0^-) \le U_{Q_1}(t_0^-) \le (k-1)L$ because $U_{Q_2} \ge 0$. On $(t_0, t]$
we have
$U_{Q_1} > (k-1)L$, so $D$ does not increase there, and $D(t) \le (k-1)L$.
Exchanging $Q_1$ and $Q_2$ gives the other direction. That the constant cannot be
lowered is the cascade of Section~\ref{sec:supp_cascade} of this file, which reaches
$(k-1)L\,(1 - ((k-1)/k)^m)$ after $m$ rounds. At $k = 1$ the bound reads
$|D| \le 0$ and is attained everywhere: one server executes work at rate $1$
whenever any job is present, so $U_{Q_1} \equiv U_{Q_2}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of the strict inequality in Lemma~\ref*{M-lem:gap}, for $k \ge 2$]
Keep the notation above, so $D = U_{Q_1} - U_{Q_2}$ is continuous and piecewise
linear with finitely many pieces. Suppose $D(t) = (k-1)L$ for some $t$ and let
$t_1$ be the smallest such $t$; it exists because $\{D = (k-1)L\}$ is closed and
$D(0) = 0 < (k-1)L$, which uses $L > 0$ and $k \ge 2$.

Let $I$ be the linear piece immediately to the left of $t_1$, which has positive
length. Its slope is strictly positive: a negative slope would put
$D > (k-1)L$ somewhere on $I$ and a zero slope would put $D = (k-1)L$ on all of
$I$, and either contradicts the minimality of $t_1$. The slope of $D$ is
$-(\min(N_{Q_1},k) - \min(N_{Q_2},k))$, so $\min(N_{Q_1},k) < \min(N_{Q_2},k)$
on $I$ and
therefore $N_{Q_1} \le k-1$ there.

With at most $k-1$ jobs present and $Q_1$ work-conserving, none of $Q_1$'s jobs
waits, so all of them are in service; hence $U_{Q_1}$ falls at rate $N_{Q_1}$ on
$I$,
and $U_{Q_1} \le (k-1)L$ on $I$ because each of the at most $k-1$ present jobs
carries at most $L$. Taking left limits at $t_1$ and using the continuity of
$D$,
\[
  U_{Q_1}(t_1^-) - U_{Q_2}(t_1^-) = (k-1)L , \qquad U_{Q_1}(t_1^-) \le (k-1)L ,
  \qquad U_{Q_2} \ge 0 ,
\]
which forces $U_{Q_2}(t_1^-) = 0$ and $U_{Q_1}(t_1^-) = (k-1)L$. Since $L > 0$
and $k \ge 2$, this
gives $N_{Q_1} \ge 1$ on $I$---with $N_{Q_1} = 0$ we would have
$U_{Q_1}(t_1^-) = 0 = (k-1)L$---so $U_{Q_1}$ is strictly decreasing on $I$ and
$U_{Q_1}(t_1^-) < \sup_I U_{Q_1} \le (k-1)L$, a contradiction. Exchanging $Q_1$
and $Q_2$ gives the other direction. Both steps that invoke $(k-1)L > 0$ fail at
$k = 1$, where the statement is false: there $U_{Q_1} \equiv U_{Q_2}$ and the
bound $0$ is attained at every instant.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Consistency, and Why the Converse Fails}
\label{sec:supp_consistency}

Section~\ref*{M-sec:theory} of the manuscript states in prose that a guard which never fires leaves the
base policy alone. Here is the statement it summarises, with its proof.

\begin{proposition}[Consistency, one direction]
\label{prop:consistency}
Run the base policy $A$ alone. If $\mathrm{over}[q](t) < \mathrm{budget}(q,t)$
for every waiting $q$ at every dispatch epoch of that run, then the wrapped run
is identical to it, with the same dispatch sequence and the same waits.
Consequently, for a constant budget $B$, each of the following implies
$\text{guard}(A,B) = A$: $\max_i \mathrm{In}^A_i < B$; or $A$ itself satisfies
$\mathrm{excess}_A[i] \le G$ on this input and $B > kG + (3k-2)L$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Induct on dispatch epochs: up to the first epoch at which the two runs could
differ their completion histories agree, hence so does $\mathrm{over}[\cdot]$,
so $E(t)$ is empty in the wrapped run and it copies $A$. The first consequence
uses $\mathrm{over}[q](t) \le \mathrm{In}^A_q$, a completed overtaker of $q$
having been dispatched while $q$ waited; the second uses Corollary~\ref*{M-cor:necessary} of the
manuscript.
\end{proof}

Consistency is therefore one-sided. A run on which no job ever reaches its budget is
the base policy's own run; a run the guard leaves unchanged need not be one.

The converse fails, so this is not an equivalence. Take $k = 1$, two jobs of
service $1$ at $t = 0$, the base policy ``higher rank first'', and $B = 1$. Both
runs dispatch in the order $(1,0)$, yet at the second dispatch the waiting job
has $\mathrm{over} = 1 \ge B$, so the guard fires and happens to choose what the
base would have chosen. Nor is there multiplicative consistency: with $k = 1$,
one job of service $L$ followed by $m$ jobs of service $1$ all at $t = 0$, the
wrapper with $B = 0$ is first-come first-served while the base ordering by
service time is not, and the ratio of mean waits is $(2L + m - 1)/(m+1)$,
unbounded in $L$.

%   of the manuscript as it then stood, moved here in the seventh revision pass under
%   referee item G3; the manuscript has carried no appendix since the ninth pass.
%   The two arguments are verbatim; only the cross-references were rewritten, since a
%   \ref into the manuscript does not resolve from this file: lem:gap is
%   Lemma 1 there, cor:necessary is Corollary 2 and the cascade is
%   Section~\ref{sec:supp_cascade}.  Simulated and checked in evidence/guard_theory; nothing was
%   recomputed for this file.


\subsection{The Net-Overtake Identity, and Its Two Directions}
\label{sec:supp_directions}

The net-overtake identity is proved here in full; the manuscript keeps the statement,
the busy-period accounting (\ref*{M-eq:busy}) and the idea. A remark follows on why its two extremes
do not have the same content, and on what the executed-work convention would change.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:identity} of the manuscript]
\emph{Busy-period accounting.} Job $i$ waits throughout $[a_i, s^P_i)$, so by
work conservation every server is busy on that interval and exactly $k\,W_P[i]$
units of work are executed there, none of it on $i$. (If $s^P_i = a_i$ the
interval is empty and both sides vanish.) Split that work by rank.

A job $j$ of rank above $i$ has $a_j \ge a_i$, so it executes inside the window
only if it is dispatched inside it, that is only if $j \prec i$; conversely every
such $j$ is dispatched at or before $s^P_i$. The work executed on jobs of rank
above $i$ is therefore $\mathrm{In}_i - \beta^{\mathrm{new}}$, where
$\beta^{\mathrm{new}}$ is the remaining work at $s^P_i$ of those overtakers not
finished by then; a same-phase overtaker contributes $C_j$ to both terms and $0$
to the executed work, as it must.

Every job of rank below $i$ is present at $a_i$, with total remaining $R^P_i$.
At $s^P_i$ their total remaining is $\mathrm{Out}_i$, the jobs not dispatched
before $i$ each carrying their full service time, plus $\beta^{\mathrm{old}}$,
the unfinished part of those dispatched strictly before $i$, so the work
executed on them is $R^P_i - \mathrm{Out}_i - \beta^{\mathrm{old}}$. Adding the
two parts gives equation~(\ref*{M-eq:busy}) of the manuscript,
$k\,W_P[i] = R^P_i + \mathrm{In}_i - \mathrm{Out}_i - \beta^P_i$ with
$\beta^P_i := \beta^{\mathrm{old}} + \beta^{\mathrm{new}}$.

\emph{Bounding $\beta$.} The jobs counted in $\beta^P_i$ occupy distinct servers,
and none of them occupies the server that takes $i$, so there are at most $k-1$
of them and each has remaining work at most $L$: $0 \le \beta^P_i \le (k-1)L$.

\emph{The reference policy.} Under first-come first-served,
$\mathrm{In}_i = 0$, since a job of rank above $i$ dispatched before $i$ would
have to be chosen at an instant at which $i$ waits, and
$\mathrm{Out}_i = 0$, since at $s^{\mathrm{FCFS}}_i$ every job of rank below $i$
has already been dispatched. So (\ref*{M-eq:busy}) reads
$k\,W_{\mathrm{FCFS}}[i] = R^{\mathrm{FCFS}}_i - \beta^{\mathrm{FCFS}}_i$ with
$0 \le \beta^{\mathrm{FCFS}}_i \le (k-1)L$.

\emph{Comparison.} At $a_i$, after the arrivals there are processed, the two
systems contain the same jobs, and those of rank $i$ or above are untouched in
both, so $U_P(a_i) - U_{\mathrm{FCFS}}(a_i) = R^P_i - R^{\mathrm{FCFS}}_i$ and
Lemma~\ref*{M-lem:gap} of the manuscript gives $|R^P_i - R^{\mathrm{FCFS}}_i| \le (k-1)L$, strictly
for $k \ge 2$. Subtracting
the two instances of (\ref*{M-eq:busy}) gives the identity. Each direction uses
only two of the three ranges: upward
$R^P_i - R^{\mathrm{FCFS}}_i \le (k-1)L$ and $\beta^{\mathrm{FCFS}}_i \le (k-1)L$
with $\beta^P_i \ge 0$, and downward the mirror image, so the slack is
$2(k-1)L$ and not $3(k-1)L$. At $k = 1$ all three terms vanish.
\end{proof}
% Ninth pass: the proof above stood in Section 6 of the manuscript, which now keeps the
%   theorem statement, the displayed busy-period equation (1) with its label, and the
%   idea.  The argument is verbatim; only the cross-references were rewritten, since a
%   \ref into the manuscript does not resolve from this file: Lemma~1 is the workload
%   comparison and (1) is the busy-period accounting, both of the manuscript.

\begin{remark}[The upward and downward extremes have different content]
\label{rem:directions}
Upward, the extremal value is a statement about delay: on the witnesses we have
computed exactly, none of $\mathrm{In}_i$ is same-phase work and the victim's own
excess is about $+3L$. Downward, half of the extremal value is an accounting
convention. There $\mathrm{In}_i = (k-1)L$ consists entirely of overtakers
dispatched in the victim's own phase, which execute nothing while it waits; the
same-phase share of $|\Delta_i|$ is \devnum{50\%} to \devnum{63\%} at
$k = 2, 3, 4$ (Section~S8.2), and the downward victim's excess is
negative, since it starts earlier under $P$ than under first-come first-served.
If $\mathrm{In}_i$ is redefined as the work of higher-ranked jobs actually
\emph{executed} during $[a_i, s^P_i)$, Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:identity} of the manuscript holds with the
same constant and the same proof, the upward supremum is still $2(k-1)L$, and
the best downward witness we have falls to $(k-1)L$. We keep the
dispatch-sequence convention for two reasons. Its constant is exactly determined
in both directions, whereas the downward constant for the executed-work variant
is open, somewhere in $((k-1)L, 2(k-1)L]$; and its two sides are symmetric.
Completed work lower-bounds both currencies, so the guard is implementable
either way: a job $j$ of rank above $i$ that completes while $i$ waits has
$a_j \ge a_i$ and is dispatched at or after $a_i$, so all of $C_j$ is executed
inside $[a_i, s^P_i)$ and is counted in full by the executed-work variant as
well.
\end{remark}

\subsection{What a Count of Overtakes Costs}
\label{sec:supp_counts}

A count bound on overtakes gives a per-job guarantee only through the worst-case charge
of $L$ per pass, and is not necessary for one. Section~\ref*{M-sec:theory} of the manuscript states that
in prose; here are the remark it summarises and the proofs of both halves.

\begin{remark}[Counting overtakes is not necessary, and is sufficient only at
the worst-case charge]
\label{rem:counts}
Bounded-overtaking mechanisms in the literature limit how many times a job may
be passed~\cite{grosof2021nudge,vanhoudt2022nudgek,grosof2022wcfs}. Inside the
model of Section~\ref*{M-sec:model} of the manuscript, where $0 < C_i \le L$, a count bound does
give a per-job guarantee; it is not necessary for one, and it gives the
guarantee only at a factor $L/\bar{C}$. Charged when an overtaker is
\emph{dispatched}, the reading a position counter in the prior art implements, a
bound of $\kappa$ passes gives $\mathrm{In}_i \le \kappa L$, and Corollary~\ref*{M-cor:sufficient} of
the manuscript turns that into
\begin{equation}
  W_P[i] \;\le\; W_{\mathrm{FCFS}}[i] + (\kappa + 2k - 2)\,L/k ,
  \label{eq:skip}
\end{equation}
which is the accounting and the formula used wherever that baseline appears in
the manuscript; inverting it, a promise $G$ admits
$\kappa = \lfloor Gk/L \rfloor - (2k-2)$ passes, the largest integer with
$(\kappa + 2k-2)L/k \le G$. Either accounting charges every overtaker the worst
case $L$, so the rule is loose against a work budget by the ratio $L/\bar{C}$,
\devnum{150} on the main trace ($L = 60$ s against a mean cost of \devnum{0.40} s).
% Source: prechecks/cost_ratio/out_mean_cost_ratio.txt (L / mean cost = 150.1, mean 0.3997 s).
\end{remark}

How the count is charged decides which guarantee. Charged when an overtaker is
\emph{dispatched}, every overtaker of $i$ goes out at an instant at which no job fires,
since a firing dispatch serves the queue head and the head is not an overtaker of $i$;
so at most $\kappa$ overtakers are dispatched while $i$ waits, which is what gives
$\mathrm{In}_i \le \kappa L$ and \eqref{eq:skip}. Charging the count at
completion instead leaves up to $k-1$ overtakers in service and the one being dispatched
uncounted, which costs a further $L$ on the right and gives the baseline fewer passes at
the same promise.
% Ninth pass: the paragraph above stood inside Remark 1 of Section 6.5 of the manuscript.
% Twelfth pass: the remark itself, with its displayed equation, moved here as well; the
%   manuscript keeps one sentence and the loss factor.

 Both halves are below. The sufficiency uses the bound on
service times, and without that bound a single
overtake makes the excess arbitrarily large: at
$t = 0$ let $k-1$ jobs of service $X$, then a job of service $\epsilon$, then one
more job of service $X$ arrive together; first-come first-served starts the
victim immediately, a policy that takes the last job instead fills every server,
and the victim waits $X$ with an overtake count of $1$. That instance steps
outside Section~\ref*{M-sec:model} of the manuscript, which fixes $C_i \le L$; inside the model the
same accounting is what makes a count bound work, with $L$ charged per pass.
Necessity fails with no such caveat: normalise to $k=1$, $L=1$ and $G=1/2$.
Let a victim of service $1$ arrive first together with $M$ short jobs, each of
service $1/(2M)$. Running the short jobs first gives every job excess at most
$1/2$, uses $\mathrm{In}_i=kG$ exactly, and lets the overtake count $M$ exceed
any fixed limit.


\subsection{Proofs of the Guard Bounds}
\label{sec:supp_guardproofs}

The additive guarantee of the wrapper holds under every budget rule that respects the
cap, and the age-relative shape carries a multiplicative form as well. Both proofs
follow.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:guard} of the manuscript]
For (\ref*{M-eq:guardin}) of the manuscript, let $j$ be the overtaker of $i$ dispatched last in the
dispatch sequence and let $t$ be its instant. Because dispatches are sequential
and $i$ is dispatched after $j$, job $i$ is waiting at the moment $j$ is
dispatched; this includes the case $t = s^P_i$, in which $j$ is dispatched in
$i$'s own phase, just before $i$. Since $j$ is dispatched, either $E(t)$ is empty
or its minimum-rank member is $j$; in both cases $i \notin E(t)$, because $i$ has
the smaller rank. Hence
$\mathrm{over}[i](t) < \mathrm{budget}(i,t) \le B_{\max}$.

The overtakers of $i$ dispatched strictly before $j$ split into those completed
by $t$, whose total service is $\mathrm{over}[i](t)$, and those still in service
at $t$; the latter occupy distinct servers, none of them the server taking $j$,
so there are at most $k-1$ of them, each of service at most $L$. Adding $j$
itself gives $\mathrm{In}_i < \mathrm{budget}(i,t) + kL \le B_{\max} + kL$.

For (\ref*{M-eq:guardadd}) of the manuscript, Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:identity} of the manuscript with
$\mathrm{Out}_i \ge 0$ gives
$k\,(W - W_{\mathrm{FCFS}}[i]) \le \mathrm{In}_i + 2(k-1)L$; substituting
(\ref*{M-eq:guardin}) of the manuscript and dividing by $k$ gives the claim, strictly. If $i$ has no
overtaker then $\mathrm{In}_i = 0$, the excess is at most $(2 - 2/k)L$, and the
bound holds strictly because $L > 0$ in the model of Section~\ref*{M-sec:model} of the manuscript.
\end{proof}

The age-relative shape is the one the manuscript names in prose. Its bound is stated
here.

\begin{proposition}[Multiplicative bound for the age-relative shape]
\label{prop:guardmult}
Suppose in addition that
$\mathrm{budget}(q,t) = \min\bigl(B_0 + \eta k (t - a_q),\ B_{\max}\bigr)$ with
one constant $B_0 \ge 0$ for every job and $\eta \in [0,1)$. Then for every
input and every job $i$,
\begin{align}
  \mathrm{In}_i &\;<\; B_0 + \eta k W + kL ,
  \label{eq:guardinmult}\\
  (1 - \eta)\,W &\;<\; W_{\mathrm{FCFS}}[i] + B_0/k + (3 - 2/k)L ,
  \label{eq:guardmult}
\end{align}
with \eqref{eq:guardinmult} asserted whenever $i$ has at least one overtaker.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The argument for (\ref*{M-eq:guardin}) of the manuscript gives
$\mathrm{In}_i < \mathrm{budget}(i,t) + kL$ at the instant $t$ of $i$'s last
overtaker, and $t \le s^P_i = a_i + W$ gives
$\mathrm{budget}(i,t) \le B_0 + \eta k W$, which is \eqref{eq:guardinmult}.
Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:identity} of the manuscript with $\mathrm{Out}_i \ge 0$ then yields
$kW - \eta k W < k W_{\mathrm{FCFS}}[i] + B_0 + (3k-2)L$; divide by $k$. Both
inequalities are strict, by the same argument as Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:guard} of the manuscript: when
$i$ has an overtaker the strictness comes from (\ref*{M-eq:guardin}) of the manuscript, and when it
has none $\mathrm{In}_i = 0$ and the excess is at most $(2-2/k)L < (3-2/k)L$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Why the Multiplicative Bound Is Claimed for One Shape Only}
\label{sec:supp_multscope}

With the queue-length shape, the multiplicative bound stated with one common constant is
false, which is why Section~\ref*{M-sec:scheduling} of the manuscript restricts the multiplicative bound to
the age-relative shape. The counterexample is below.

\begin{remark}[Why the multiplicative bound is claimed for one shape only]
\label{rem:mult_scope}
The same computation goes through when $B_0$ is replaced by a quantity
$B_{0,i}$ fixed at $i$'s own arrival, and then bounds $W$ in terms of
$B_{0,i}$. The queue-length shape of Section~\ref*{M-sec:scheduling} of the manuscript sets
$B_{0,q} = B_0 + \gamma n_q$ with $n_q$ the number of jobs waiting when $q$
arrives, so $B_{0,i}$ varies from job to job. Stated with the common $B_0$ the
multiplicative bound is then false, by a margin that grows with $\gamma$. Take
$k = 1$, $B_0 = 0$, $B_{\max} = \gamma$, any $\eta \in [0,1)$, and the input: a
job of service $L$ at $t = 0$, which is dispatched at once; a second job of
service $L$ at $t = 1$, which waits with $n_q = 0$; the victim $i$, also of
service $L$, at $t = 1$ and ranked after it, so that $i$ arrives behind a single
waiting job, has $n_i = 1$ and hence budget $\gamma$; and $\gamma$ jobs of
service $1$ at $t = 2L$, all ranked above $i$. First-come first-served runs the
first two jobs on $[0,L]$ and $[L,2L]$ and starts $i$ at $2L$, so
$W_{\mathrm{FCFS}}[i] = 2L-1$. Under the wrapper the second job is taken at
$t = L$ as well, by the guard if $\eta = 0$ and by the base otherwise; at
$t = 2L$ the victim has
$\mathrm{over}[i] = 0 < \gamma$, so the guard does not fire and the base spends
the whole budget on the stream, giving $W_P[i] = 2L - 1 + \gamma$ against a
right-hand side of $(3L-1)/(1-\eta)$. The bound is violated for every
$\gamma > (3L-1)/(1-\eta) - (2L-1)$, that is for $\gamma > L$ at $\eta = 0$, and
the margin grows without bound in $\gamma$. The same happens for
every per-job constant. For $\gamma > 0$ we therefore claim
Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:guard} of the manuscript and not Proposition~\ref{prop:guardmult}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Proofs of the Necessity Pair}
\label{sec:supp_necessity}

A work budget is necessary for a per-job guarantee, up to an additive $O(kL)$. The two
statements that make that argument, Lemma~\ref*{M-lem:outbound} and Corollary~\ref*{M-cor:necessary} of the manuscript, are
proved here.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma~\ref*{M-lem:outbound} of the manuscript]
Every job $j$ counted in $\mathrm{Out}_i$ has rank below $i$, so first-come
first-served dispatches it before $i$ and $s^P_j \le s^{\mathrm{FCFS}}_j + G \le
s^{\mathrm{FCFS}}_i + G$; it is also dispatched at or after $s^P_i$. All of them
therefore start inside a window of length
$w = G - \mathrm{excess}_P[i] \ge 0$. Of the jobs one server starts inside
such a window, all but the last complete inside it, so their total service is at
most $w$ and the last adds at most $L$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Corollary~\ref*{M-cor:necessary} of the manuscript]
Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:identity} of the manuscript gives
$\mathrm{In}_i - \mathrm{Out}_i \le k\,\mathrm{excess}_P[i] + 2(k-1)L$; add
Lemma~\ref*{M-lem:outbound} of the manuscript, whose hypothesis holds because the guarantee is
assumed for every job, and the terms in $\mathrm{excess}_P[i]$ cancel.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The Floor of Order \texorpdfstring{$L$}{L}, and the Scope of Its Hypothesis}
\label{sec:supp_floorscope}

The floor of Proposition~\ref*{M-prop:floor} of the manuscript is proved here, and its hypothesis is shown
to be neither droppable nor tightenable, the remaining cases of the budget family being
exhaustive.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref*{M-prop:floor} of the manuscript]
Into an empty system, at $t=0$, let $k+1$ jobs arrive together, all of service
$L$. The first $k$ ranks are precisely the jobs FCFS starts at once. Nothing has
completed, so every waiting job has $\mathrm{over}=0$; because its first-epoch
budget is positive, no waiting job fires and the wrapper accepts the base
policy's choices. At the first choice of a job beyond the first $k$ ranks, that
job displaces one of FCFS's first $k$ equal-length jobs. The displaced job waits
until a completion under the wrapper but starts at zero under FCFS, and hence has
excess $L$. A prediction-ranked base produces the same choice when one of the
later ranks receives the preferred score. At $k=1$ and $B>0$,
(\ref*{M-eq:guardin}) of the manuscript gives $\mathrm{excess}<B+L$ strictly, and a family approaching
it is in Section~S8.4; $B=0$ is FCFS.
\end{proof}

Two remarks on the hypothesis, because the proposition is easy to state too
widely and too narrowly. It cannot be dropped: as a claim about policies the
statement is false, since first-come first-served is non-preemptive,
work-conserving, reads no service time before completion, and has excess $0$ on
every input, as does any rank-ordered policy. For the same reason it is false as
a claim about every base policy: the guard run around first-come first-served is
first-come first-served, whatever the budget. The force comes from the guard's
not firing, which is a property of the budget rule and not of work conservation,
together with a base policy that uses the freedom this leaves it.
It is also not necessary. A rule that is $0$ at the first dispatch epoch and
positive later, such as the age-relative shape with $B_0 = 0$ and $\eta > 0$,
falls to the same instance placed behind a saturating prefix: let $k$ jobs
of service $L$ arrive at $t = 0$, the victim just after them, and $k$ further
jobs just before $t = L$. The victim's wait up to $t = L$ is what first-come
first-served gives it, its budget at $t = L$ is positive because it has aged,
and the $k$ newcomers then take every server. The remaining case is degenerate:
if the rule gives budget $0$ to a job whenever no completed work has overtaken
it, then at every dispatch epoch the lowest-ranked waiting job $h$ has
$\mathrm{over}[h] = 0$, so $h$ lies in $E(t)$ and is its minimum-rank member and
is dispatched, and by induction the wrapper is first-come first-served itself,
whatever the base policy. For the budget of Algorithm~\ref*{M-alg:guard} of the manuscript these cases
are exhaustive. If $B_{\max} = 0$ or $B_0 = \gamma = \eta = 0$ the budget is
identically zero and the wrapper is first-come first-served. If $B_0 > 0$ the
proposition applies, and if $B_0 = 0 < \eta$ the saturating prefix does. If
$B_0 = \eta = 0 < \gamma$, let $k+2$ jobs of service $L$ arrive together at
$t = 0$: the first has $n_q = 0$, hence budget $0$, and is released at once,
every other job has a positive budget and nothing has overtaken it, so a base
policy that prefers the highest ranks passes over the second job at $t = 0$ when
$k \ge 2$, and at $t = L$ when $k = 1$, and that job's excess is $L$. A budget
rule of this family therefore either makes the wrapper first-come first-served
around every base policy, or admits a base policy and an input with excess at
least $L$.
