\subsection{Why the Budget Is Measured in Work and Not in Positions}
\label{sec:supp_workpositions}

A count of passes is the currency of the finite-skip and Nudge-$K$
rules~\cite{grosof2021nudge,vanhoudt2022nudgek,grosof2022wcfs}. Counting is not
necessary: a job can be passed any number of times and stay inside its promise, provided
the work that passes it is small. Counting is sufficient in this model, but only through
the worst-case charge of $L$ per overtake, since every overtaker is bounded by the time
limit; a count of $\kappa$ therefore buys a promise of $(\kappa + 2k-2)L/k$
(Remark~\ref{rem:counts} and \eqref{eq:skip}). That is the bound a count can give, and it
is loose by the ratio $L/\bar{C}$, \devnum{150} on the main trace. Ten
% Source: prechecks/cost_ratio/out_mean_cost_ratio.txt (L / mean cost = 150.1, mean 0.3997 s).
overtakers that each run for the full limit $L$ and ten that run for ten milliseconds
carry the same count and delays that differ by three orders of magnitude, so the charge
is paid on every dispatch whether or not the work is there.
Section~\ref*{M-sec:experiments} of the manuscript runs the count rule as a baseline and measures how much
the looseness costs on a real load. That work is the currency the excess is actually
written in is the content of the identity of Section~\ref*{M-sec:theory} of the manuscript, not a claim of
ours about what earlier mechanisms could not do.

\subsection{Which Assumptions Can Be Dropped}
\label{sec:supp_relaxations}

Four of the assumptions behind Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:identity} and Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:guard} of the manuscript can be
dropped, and one cannot. The global bound on service times goes first, and it is the
one whose replacement has to be read at the right instant.

\begin{proposition}[The prefix maximum replaces the global bound]
\label{prop:supp_prefix_max}
Write $\Lambda(t) = \max\{C_j : a_j \le t\}$ for the largest service time among the
jobs that have arrived by $t$. No global bound on service times is needed, provided
$\Lambda$ is read at the right instant, and the right instant is not the same for
every statement:
\begin{enumerate}
\item the upward direction of Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:identity} of the manuscript, and hence Corollary~\ref*{M-cor:sufficient} of
the manuscript, hold with $L$ replaced by $\Lambda(a_i)$;
\item the wrapper bounds (\ref*{M-eq:guardin}) and (\ref*{M-eq:guardadd}) of the manuscript, and likewise
\eqref{eq:guardinmult} and \eqref{eq:guardmult},
hold in the sharp forms
$\mathrm{In}_i < B_{\max} + k\,\Lambda(s^P_i)$ and
$\mathrm{excess}_P[i] < B_{\max}/k + \Lambda(s^P_i) + (2-2/k)\,\Lambda(a_i)$, and
since $\Lambda(a_i) \le \Lambda(s^P_i)$ they give (\ref*{M-eq:guardadd}) with $\Lambda(s^P_i)$
throughout; with $\Lambda(a_i)$ in place of $\Lambda(s^P_i)$ both are false, and by
an unbounded margin;
\item the downward direction of Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:identity} of the manuscript holds with
$\Lambda(\max(s^P_i, s^{\mathrm{FCFS}}_i))$, and Corollary~\ref*{M-cor:necessary} of the manuscript holds
with $\Lambda(s^{\mathrm{FCFS}}_i + G)$; both are false with $\Lambda(a_i)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

Item 1 holds because every job counted in $R^P_i$, $R^{\mathrm{FCFS}}_i$ or
$\beta^{\mathrm{FCFS}}_i$ has rank below $i$ and so arrived by $a_i$. Item 2 needs the
later instant because the $kL$ term of (\ref*{M-eq:guardadd}) charges the last overtaker and the at most
$k-1$ overtakers still in service beside it, all of them jobs of rank above $i$, which
arrive after $a_i$ and are bounded only by the prefix maximum at the instant $i$
starts. The counterexample at the earlier instant is short. Take $k = 1$ with budget
$B = 2$, let $i$ and one job of service $1$ arrive at $t = 0$ and a job of service $M$
at $t = 1$, and let the base policy take the two others first. Then
$\Lambda(a_i) = 1$ for every $M$, the guard never fires because $\mathrm{over}[i]$
stops at $1 < 2$, and $\mathrm{In}_i = M + 1$ against a claimed
$B + k\Lambda(a_i) = 3$. In item 3 the horizon
$\Lambda(\max(s^P_i, s^{\mathrm{FCFS}}_i))$ is not claimed to be the smallest that
works, and $\Lambda(s^{\mathrm{FCFS}}_i + G)$ is what Corollary~\ref*{M-cor:necessary} needs because
Lemma~\ref*{M-lem:outbound} of the manuscript bounds jobs that start as late as that.

The remaining four are shorter, and the last of them is the one that fails.

\begin{proposition}[Relaxations]
\label{prop:relax}
In the setting of Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:identity} and Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:guard} of the manuscript:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Servers may have different speeds.} With speeds $v_1, \dots, v_k$
and $V = \sum_{\ell} v_{\ell}$, Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:identity} of the manuscript holds with $k$ replaced by
$V$ on the left and the same $2(k-1)L$ on the right, and the guard satisfies
$W_P[i] \le W_{\mathrm{FCFS}}[i] + B/V + (3k-2)L/V$: the budget is divided by
total capacity, not by the server count.
Here $L$ bounds a job's work in a common unit, not its wall-clock duration: a common
wall-clock limit does not produce policy-independent executed work when speeds
differ. The reference assigns each dispatched job to the fastest free server, ties
by server index.
\item \emph{The timeout is already in the model.} Section~\ref*{M-sec:model} of the manuscript defines
$C_j$ as executed work, $\min(C_j, L)$ for a job stopped at the limit, so every
currency above ($\mathrm{In}_i$, $\mathrm{Out}_i$, $\mathrm{over}[q](t)$ and
$L$) is in executed work and no assumption on raw service demands is needed.
Read in raw work the statements are false, a stopped job contributing more to
$\mathrm{In}_i$ than is ever executed.
\item \emph{Setup times survive.} If a setup of length $\theta$ is paid before
every service under both policies, absorb it into the job as
$C'_j = C_j + \theta$; the model is unchanged and every result holds with
$L \mapsto L + \theta$.
\item \emph{Work conservation cannot be dropped.} If servers may idle while jobs
wait, let $\Gamma^P_i$ be the server-idle time accumulated during
$[a_i, s^P_i)$ while at least one job waited. Then equation~(\ref*{M-eq:busy}) of the manuscript
becomes
$k W_P[i] = R^P_i + \mathrm{In}_i - \mathrm{Out}_i - \beta^P_i + \Gamma^P_i$, and
nothing survives without a bound on $\Gamma$: with $k = 1$, pausing the server
for $T$ while $i$ waits leaves $\mathrm{In}_i = \mathrm{Out}_i = 0$ and makes
the excess $T$. Release constraints are the same phenomenon: if rank is keyed
on arrival while eligibility starts later, the identity fails by exactly the
forced idle time, and ranking on release repairs it.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

Only the last of the four changes what can be promised. The other three change the
constant, the unit or nothing at all.
% Ninth to eleventh passes: these two propositions stood in Section 6.9 of the
%   manuscript, Section 6.7 since the twelfth pass, as one proposition
%   "Relaxations", whose item 1 is now the first of the
%   two.  The manuscript keeps a one-paragraph summary and this pointer; the
%   mathematics, the sharp forms and the counterexample are unchanged.

\subsection{A Clock in Place of the Counter, and an Absolute Bound}
\label{sec:supp_clock}

This section proves Propositions~\ref*{M-prop:clock} and~\ref*{M-prop:absolute} of the manuscript and the combined rule stated
after Proposition~\ref*{M-prop:clock}. We keep the conventions of Section~\ref*{M-sec:conventions} of the manuscript: a phase
is the ordered list of dispatches at one instant, and remaining work at $a_i$ is read
after the arrivals at $a_i$.

\begin{lemma}[Fluid comparison]
\label{lem:fluid}
Let $V(t)$ be the unfinished work at $t$ of a single server of rate $k$ fed the same
jobs at the same instants, serving whenever it holds work. For every non-preemptive
work-conserving $k$-server policy $Q$ and every $t$,
$V(t) \le U_Q(t) \le V(t) + (k-1)L$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Both quantities rise by $C_j$ at the arrival of $j$, so $D = U_Q - V$ changes only
between arrivals. There $U_Q$ falls at the number of busy servers and $V$ falls at
rate $k$ while it is positive. If $V > 0$, the slope of $D$ is $k$ minus the number of
busy servers, which is non-negative; if $V = 0$, then $D = U_Q \ge 0$. Since $D = 0$
before the first arrival, $D \ge 0$ throughout. If $D > (k-1)L$, then
$U_Q > (k-1)L$. All $k$ servers are then busy, since otherwise the queue is empty by
work conservation and the at most $k-1$ jobs in service hold at most $(k-1)L$. So
$U_Q$ falls at rate $k$ and $D$ does not rise. Hence $D \le (k-1)L$ throughout.
\end{proof}

Write $V_i^-$ for the fluid backlog at $a_i$ of the jobs of rank below $i$, as defined
in Section~\ref*{M-sec:absolute} of the manuscript. No dispatch happens between two arrivals at the same
instant, and an arrival moves $U_Q$ and $V$ by the same amount. Reading $D$ at $a_i$
after the arrivals of rank below $i$ and before $i$'s own gives $U_Q = R^Q_i$ and
$V = V_i^-$, so Lemma~\ref{lem:fluid} yields $R^Q_i \le V_i^- + (k-1)L$ for every
policy $Q$. A clairvoyant policy is included, since the lemma is a statement about the
schedule that was actually run.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref*{M-prop:absolute} of the manuscript]
Equation~(\ref*{M-eq:busy}) of the manuscript gives
$k\,W_P[i] = R^P_i + \mathrm{In}_i - \mathrm{Out}_i - \beta^P_i \le R^P_i + \mathrm{In}_i$,
and $R^P_i \le V_i^- + (k-1)L$. Under first-come first-served $\mathrm{In}_i = 0$.
Under Algorithm~\ref*{M-alg:guard}, $\mathrm{In}_i < B_{\max} + kL$ when $i$ has an overtaker, by
Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:guard} of the manuscript, and $\mathrm{In}_i = 0$ otherwise. Dividing by $k$ gives
the first bound; $V_i^- \le \sigma - C_i < \sigma$ gives the second. That $\sigma$ is the
least depth of a conforming rate-$k$ bucket is the usual reading of the Lindley
recursion~\cite{cruz1991calculus}: $V_i^- + C_i$ is the largest amount by which the work
arriving in a closed window ending at $a_i$ exceeds $k$ times the window's length.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref*{M-prop:clock} of the manuscript]
Let $i$ have an overtaker. At every dispatch epoch $t \ge a_i + \theta$ at which $i$
waits, the minimum-rank waiting job has rank at most $i$ and has waited at least
$t - a_i \ge \theta$, so it is served; no job of rank above $i$ is dispatched at or
after $a_i + \theta$ while $i$ waits. Every overtaker therefore starts in
$[a_i, a_i + \theta)$ and ends no later than $L$ after its start. The overtakers on one
server run one after another inside $[a_i, a_i + \theta + L)$, the last starting
strictly before $a_i + \theta$, so their total service is below $\theta + L$. Summing
over the $k$ servers, $\mathrm{In}_i < k(\theta + L)$. Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:identity} of the manuscript with
$\mathrm{Out}_i \ge 0$ gives
$k(W - W_{\mathrm{FCFS}}[i]) \le \mathrm{In}_i + 2(k-1)L < k\theta + (3k-2)L$, and the
first inequality of Proposition~\ref*{M-prop:absolute} gives the absolute form
$W < V_i^-/k + \theta + (2-1/k)L$. A job without an overtaker has
$\mathrm{In}_i = 0$ and excess at most $(2-2/k)L$.
\end{proof}

The reading of ``oldest'' as minimum rank is needed. If jobs that arrive at the same
instant are treated as equally old and the base policy chooses among them, the step
``serves a job of rank below $i$'' fails. At $k = 1$, $L = 3$ and $\theta = 0$, six jobs
arrive at $t = 0$ with sizes $1, 3, 3, 3, 3, 3$. A base policy that serves the five
long jobs first makes job~1 wait $15$ s with $W_{\mathrm{FCFS}} = 0$, against the bound
$\theta + L = 3$.

\begin{proof}[Proof of the combined rule]
The rule serves the minimum-rank waiting job if it has waited $\theta$, and otherwise
runs Algorithm~\ref*{M-alg:guard}. Suppose $j$ is served while $i$, of lower rank, waits. Either the
base policy chose $j$, in which case $E(t)$ is empty, or $j$ is the minimum-rank member
of $E(t)$, in which case $i \notin E(t)$. In both cases
$\mathrm{over}[i](t) < \mathrm{budget}(i,t)$, which is the only property of the
dispatch the proof of Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:guard} uses; so Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:guard} holds. When the minimum-rank job has
waited $\theta$ it is served, which is the only property the proof of Proposition~\ref*{M-prop:clock}
uses; so Proposition~\ref*{M-prop:clock} holds. The second argument reads no completion, so it holds for
charges that arrive late or never.
\end{proof}

The order of the two tests matters whenever budgets differ between jobs. Serving the
minimum-rank member of $E(t)$ first can pass over an aged head, and it breaks
Proposition~\ref*{M-prop:clock}. At $k = 1$, $L = 3$, $\theta = 3.5$ and
$\mathrm{budget}(q) = \min(3 n_q, 5)$, jobs arrive at $0, 0, 0, 2, 2, 2$ with sizes
$1, 1, 1, 3, 1, 3$, so their budgets are $0, 3, 5, 3, 5, 5$. The base policy serves
jobs 1, 2, 5 and~6 in that order, and at $t = 6$ both job~3 and job~4 have
$\mathrm{over} = 4$. Job~3 has waited $6 \ge \theta$ but is below its budget, and job~4
has passed its budget. Serving job~4 first makes job~3 start at $9$, with
$W_{\mathrm{FCFS}} = 2$ and excess $7 > \theta + L = 6.5$; serving the aged head first
leaves every excess at most $4$. The age-relative shape gives a second instance, at
$k = 1$, $\theta = 0$, $\eta = 1/4$, arrivals $0, 1, 2$ and sizes $2, 3, 3$. With a
constant budget the fired set is a prefix of the queue and the two orders coincide.

We checked every statement of this section by exhaustive enumeration and by random
search, with two simulators written independently of each other and of the package. The
enumeration covered $k = 1, 2, 3$, up to six jobs of sizes in $\{1, 2, 3\}$ with
$L = 3$, arrivals in $\{0, \dots, 4\}$, and every choice a base policy can make at every
dispatch, so clairvoyant and randomised base policies are included. It ran 53 to 56
million schedules per $k$. The random search covered 320{,}000 bursty instances with
$k \le 4$ and up to $40$ jobs. It found no violation of Lemma~\ref{lem:fluid}, of
Proposition~\ref*{M-prop:absolute} for any policy, of Proposition~\ref*{M-prop:clock} under the rank reading, or of the combined
rule under late or lost reports; every failure found came from the two readings the
text excludes, ties broken by the base policy and serving the fired set before an aged
head, and the instances above are the smallest found. The worst observed ratio of the excess
to $\theta + (3-2/k)L$ was $0.996$ at $k = 1$ and between $0.756$ and $0.829$ for
$k = 2, 3, 4$; for $k \ge 2$ the constant may be loose, and we do not claim it is exact.
% Sources: prechecks/new_theory_refutation/refutation.md (verdicts, the two witnesses,
%   the search space and the table of worst ratios); out_refute_exhaustive.txt,
%   out_refute_random.txt, out_refute_crosscheck.txt, out_refute_eta_witness.json,
%   out_refute_witness.json key "GFEX k=1"; prechecks/timeout_rule/out_theory_check.txt
%   (the author's own check: 571,776 exhaustive and 200,000 random cases, 0 violations).

\subsection{Completion Reports That Arrive Late}
\label{sec:supp_late}

This section proves Proposition~\ref*{M-prop:late} of the manuscript. The charge of a job $c$ that
completes at $f_c$ reaches the shared counter at $f_c + d_c$, with
$0 \le d_c \le \delta$, and is added to every job of rank below $c$ still waiting then.
At one instant the order is completions, charges due by then, arrivals, dispatches, so
$\delta = 0$ is Algorithm~\ref*{M-alg:guard} exactly. Servers still pull the instant they free.

\begin{proof}[Proof of the first part]
Fix $i$ with an overtaker, let $j$ be its last overtaker, $t$ the instant of $j$'s
dispatch and $s^*$ the server taking $j$. As in Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:guard} of the manuscript,
$i \notin E(t)$, so $\mathrm{over}[i](t) < B_{\max}$. Every charge added to
$\mathrm{over}[i]$ comes from an overtaker, since a job of higher rank that completes
while $i$ waits was dispatched before $i$. Split the overtakers dispatched before $j$
into (a) those in service at $t$, (b) those completed and charged by $t$, and (c) those
completed by $t$ whose charge has not arrived, so $f_c \in (t - \delta, t]$. Then
$\mathrm{In}_i = (a) + (b) + (c) + C_j$ with $(b) < B_{\max}$. The jobs in (a) sit on
distinct servers other than $s^*$, so $(a) \le (k-1)L$. On one server $s$, the jobs in
(c) do not overlap in time. Let $c_1$ be the one that finishes first. Then
$C_{c_1} \le L$, and every other job of (c) on $s$ starts at or after
$f_{c_1} > t - \delta$ and finishes by $t$, so together they hold less than $\delta$.
Hence (c) holds less than $L + \delta$ per server, and
$\mathrm{In}_i < B_{\max} + (k-1)L + k(L + \delta) + L$. Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:identity} of the manuscript
with $\mathrm{Out}_i \ge 0$ gives
$k(W - W_{\mathrm{FCFS}}[i]) < B_{\max} + (3k-2)L + k(L + \delta)$.
\end{proof}

The extra $L + \delta$ cannot be charged against (a): one server can hold a stale job of
size $L$ and an overtaker in service at the same instant. At $k = 1$ the following
instance attains the integer form $B + 2L + \delta - 2$ of the bound. All jobs arrive
at $0$. The victim has rank~1, size $L$ and the worst score. Then come $B - 1$ unit jobs
charged at once, one job of size $L$ and $\delta - 1$ unit jobs all charged after
$\delta$, and a last job of size $L$, scored in that order. When the last unit
finishes, $\mathrm{over} = B - 1 < B$, the last job starts, and the victim starts
$B + 2L + \delta - 2$ after its FCFS start. For $k \ge 2$ we start from the cascade of Section~S8.1 with $L = k^m$, delay the charges
of its final jobs by $\delta$, let each server run $\delta - 1$ unit jobs charged late, add
a second batch of $k$ jobs of size $L$, and set $B = f + L + 1$. At $k = 2$ the victim's
excess is then $f + 3L + \delta - 1$, two units below the bound at every depth $m$, so the
bound is attained in the limit. At $m = 12$ the ratio is at least $0.99988$ for
$\delta = 1, 2048, 4096$, and at $m = 8$ the excess exceeds Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:guard}'s bound by
$L + \delta - 2$. On the same family at $k = 3$ and $k = 4$, every instance we built
falls short of the bound by $(1 - 1/k)L((k-1)/k)^m + 1 + 1/k$, a vanishing fraction of
$L$ as $m$ grows; we have no proof of that formula. At $\delta = 0$ the rule is
Algorithm~\ref*{M-alg:guard} and Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:guard} gives the smaller bound, so attainment needs $\delta > 0$.

\begin{proof}[Proof of the second part]
Suppose every server applies its own completion report in the same step as its next
pull. Take $t$, $j$ and $s^*$ as above, and let $c$ be a stale overtaker on server $s$.
Every earlier pull of $s$ applied the report of the job $s$ had just finished, so $c$ is
the last job $s$ finished and $s$ has not pulled since $f_c$. If $f_c < t$, then at the
instant $f_c$ the server $s$ was free while $i$ waited, so by work conservation it
pulled at $f_c$ and applied $c$'s report, a contradiction. So $f_c = t$, and $s$ has
not pulled yet in the phase at $t$. Then $s \ne s^*$, because $s^*$ applied its own
report before pulling $j$, and $s$ holds no job in service. Per server other than
$s^*$, (a) and (c) together are at most one job of size at most $L$, and on $s^*$ both
are empty. So $\mathrm{In}_i < B_{\max} + kL$, which is inequality~(\ref*{M-eq:guardin}) of the
manuscript, and Theorem~\ref*{M-thm:guard} follows as before. Nothing in the argument reads another
server's report, so it holds for every $\delta$, and for reports that never arrive.
\end{proof}

A third arrangement keeps one view of the counter per server, in which a server sees
its own completions at once and other servers' within $\delta$. The same argument then
gives $\mathrm{excess} < B_{\max}/k + (3 - 2/k)L + (1 - 1/k)(L + \delta)$. At $k = 2$ a
variant of the cascade falls short of it by $3/2$ at every depth, so this bound too is
attained in the limit.

We checked the three statements in two ways, with no violation in either. The first
enumeration fixed static priority keys, constant budgets $B \in \{1, 2, 3\}$ and
$\delta \in \{0, 1, 2\}$ at $k = 1, 2$, and took every key order and delay vector for up
to four jobs and a sample of them for five and six: $720{,}894{,}276$ schedules, plus
$200{,}000$ random instances with $k \le 4$. The second, written independently, lets the
adversary take any dispatch the budget allows, which covers dynamic and clairvoyant
base policies. It also chooses when each charge arrives within $\delta$, the order in
which servers pull at one instant, and, under report-before-pull, whether a report
arrives at all. It enumerated every such schedule for $k = 1, 2, 3$ and up to six jobs,
about $1.4$ billion in all, and a hill-climbing search over up to $40$ jobs and $k \le 4$
added $15.4$ million instances. The combined rule of Section~\ref{sec:supp_clock}, run
with lost reports, had no violation either.
% Sources: prechecks/stale_charging/derivation.md, Sections 1-7 (proofs and the
%   lower-bound instances); out_sim_stale.txt:10-85 (instances, ratios 0.9980-0.9987 at
%   k = 2, m = 8, L = 256) and :87-108 (200,000 random instances);
%   out_sim_stale_exhaustive_n6.txt:14-36 (2,273,660,361 schedules, 0 violations, of which
%   720,894,276 are models U, A1, A2 and the rest the clock-union model UN);
%   maximal_adversary/out_exhaustive.txt (shared 1.03e9, report-before-pull 5.06e7,
%   per-server 3.49e8 decision paths), out_hill_a.txt and out_hill_b.txt (5.58e6 + 5.20e6
%   + 4.58e6 evaluations), out_construct.txt (k = 2, m = 12 ratios 0.99988-0.99990; k = 3,
%   4 gaps) and out_construct_pserv.txt (per-server gap 3/2);
%   out_check_union_rule.txt.

\section{Reproducibility and Provenance of the Empirical Results}
\label{sec:supp_repro}

\subsection{Feature Definitions, Targets and Rolling Splits}

The executable feature dictionary is \path{src/spjf_guard/features/sweep.py}:
\path{HIST_COLS}, \path{CTX_COLS}, \path{GROUPS} and
\path{feature_frame} specify the entity aggregates, missing values and event
order; \path{src/spjf_guard/data/events.py} specifies code columns and clock inputs.
M4 combines static code, assessment/calendar context, exercise history, user history,
user--exercise history and previous-event history. It excludes the relational
extensions. Outcome aggregates contain log-cost moments and quantiles, error/heavy
rates and recent-result summaries; counts and elapsed time since arrival are known
at arrival and are distinguished from outcome fields. The rolling-history window,
tie order and update formulas are defined in that dictionary. Static uses only the
code and context blocks. The independent source-clock audit in Section~S1 verifies
that dictionary under its original clock, not under an arbitrary replayed policy.

Package scores are fitted by \path{src/spjf_guard/predict/forward.py}. For each
target term, training includes only terms whose first arrival precedes that target,
and only records whose result is readable strictly before its first arrival.
History thresholds and user-slowness cutpoints are refitted from those training
rows; a model is then frozen for the target term. The targets are
\devnum{2020-ERE}, \devnum{2020-2}, \devnum{2021-1}, \devnum{2021-2},
\devnum{2022-1} and \devnum{2022-2}. Starting at \devnum{2018-1}, their training
periods end respectively at \devnum{2019-2}, \devnum{2020-1}, \devnum{2020-2},
\devnum{2021-1}, \devnum{2021-2} and \devnum{2022-1}; availability filtering still
applies at each cutoff. Validation overlays exclude the final development term
from selection, but that term and the earlier family exploration are development
data, not sealed testing.
% Sources: outputs/dev_predictor/manifest.json, arguments.targets;
% evidence/ranking_score/fit_targets.csv, target and train_sems; outputs/selection_v3/
% manifest.json, validation pool; prechecks/heldout_scope/heldout_scope.md, Q2.

The score objectives are in \path{src/spjf_guard/predict/scores.py}, and the
deterministic fit settings in \texttt{lgb\_parameters} and the configuration
identified by the output manifest. \texttt{spjf\_e} is a Tweedie regression of
capped seconds; \texttt{spjf\_log} is squared-error regression of log-transformed
cost. In contrast, the earlier M4 AUROC \devnum{0.9283 [0.9118, 0.9415]} is the
heavy-class classifier probability, averaged over \devnum{3} seeds, trained on
\devnum{2018-1}--\devnum{2019-2} and tested only on \devnum{2022-2}. Those
retrospective classifier predictions never enter scheduling. The package's pooled
\texttt{spjf\_e} AUROC \devnum{0.9360} combines \devnum{402{,}358} unreplicated
forward-prediction rows. Its evaluation heavy threshold stays at the core training
p95, \devnum{1.559043} s; it is not the threshold refitted inside each model's
history features. Predictor intervals resample users, whereas scheduling intervals
resample weeks. The two AUROCs compare different fits, targets and pooling and
must not be read as a measured improvement on a common split.
% Sources: evidence/codebench_service_v2/out_service_v2.txt, SUMMARY 1;
% outputs/dev_predictor/predictor_metrics.csv, pooled/spjf_e; manifest.json,
% heavy_reference_semesters, heavy_threshold_s and bootstrap_block.

\subsection{Exact Selection Grids and Counts}

The full expanded grid, its definitions, counts and deduplication totals are in
\path{outputs/selection_v3/selection_protocol.json}; per-cell values, worst-cell
values and selections are in the adjacent \path{selection_grid.csv},
\path{selection_worst.csv}, \path{selection_frontier.csv} and
\path{selected_parameters.csv}. All budget and queue-work bases below scale
by $k/\devnum{4}$ before dispatch arithmetic. The cap is determined by the promise.
% Source: outputs/selection_v3/selection_protocol.json and
% outputs/dev_tables/policy_parameters.csv, k and B0/gamma values.

The constant grid has the following \devnum{39} base budgets (seconds), shared
across promises:
\begin{quote}\small
\devnum{15}, \devnum{17.99}, \devnum{21.576}, \devnum{25.877}, \devnum{30}, \devnum{31.035}, \devnum{37.222}, \devnum{44.641}, \devnum{53.54}, \devnum{60}, \devnum{64.212}, \devnum{77.012}, \devnum{92.363}, \devnum{110.774}, \devnum{120}, \devnum{132.855}, \devnum{159.338}, \devnum{191.099}, \devnum{229.192}, \devnum{240}, \devnum{274.878}, \devnum{329.671}, \devnum{360}, \devnum{395.386}, \devnum{474.201}, \devnum{480}, \devnum{568.726}, \devnum{600}, \devnum{682.093}, \devnum{818.058}, \devnum{981.126}, \devnum{1176.7}, \devnum{1411.26}, \devnum{1692.57}, \devnum{2029.96}, \devnum{2434.6}, \devnum{2919.9}, \devnum{3501.94}, \devnum{4200}.
\end{quote}
% Source: outputs/selection_v3/selection_protocol.json, expanded_points, fixed with promise_s=-1.

It is the union of \devnum{32} logarithmically spaced values between
\devnum{15} and \devnum{4200} s, rounded as recorded, and the anchors
$\devnum{15},\devnum{30},\devnum{60},\devnum{120},\devnum{240},\devnum{360},
\devnum{480},\devnum{600}$, with duplicate values removed. A full-cap constant
point is added at each of $G=\devnum{300},\devnum{600},\devnum{1200}$ s.
This makes \devnum{42} expanded constant entries.
% Source: outputs/selection_v3/selection_protocol.json, definition.fixed and expanded_points.

For each promise the age-relative grid is the Cartesian product
\[
 B_{0,\mathrm{base}}\in\{\devnum{0},\devnum{15},\devnum{30},\devnum{60},
 \devnum{120},\devnum{240},\devnum{360},\devnum{480},\devnum{600}\},\qquad
 \eta\in\{\devnum{0},\devnum{0.25},\devnum{0.5},\devnum{0.75},
 \devnum{0.9},\devnum{0.95}\},\quad\gamma=0.
\]
The queue-length grid is
\[
 B_{0,\mathrm{base}}\in\{\devnum{0},\devnum{30},\devnum{120}\},\qquad
 \eta\in\{\devnum{0},\devnum{0.9}\},\qquad
 \gamma_{\mathrm{base}}\in\{\devnum{1},\devnum{4},\devnum{16}\}.
\]
Thus the family counts over all promises are
$\devnum{42}+\devnum{162}+\devnum{54}=\devnum{258}$ expanded points.
Deduplicating identical dispatch rules after scaling and saturation leaves
\devnum{243} distinct schedules at each evaluated server count. This is a
computational deduplication, not a reduction of the declared search space.
% Source: outputs/selection_v3/selection_protocol.json, definition.capped/hybrid,
% expanded_points and per_server_count.

Candidate counts per promise answer a different question. The constant entries
shared across promises qualify for a given $G$ only if their worst-cell certified
promise is at most $G$; an age-relative or queue-length entry belongs to its own
declared promise. Feasibility then additionally requires harm $\le G/2$ in every
validation cell. Table~\ref{tab:s_grid_counts} separates these filters, explaining
why the per-promise totals differ from the expanded search size. The objective is
the minimum deadline gap over all validation cells, with ties resolved by smaller
worst harm, then smaller $B_0$, $\eta$ and $\gamma$.

\devnum{0.00001}, \devnum{0.00003}, \devnum{0.0001}, \devnum{0.0003},

\subsection{Replication, History and Aggregation Order}

Each overlay is built from the same \devnum{40} class-terms replicated
\devnum{44} times with independent whole-week shifts; each of the five overlays
therefore reuses the same jobs. Original scores are computed on the source calendar
and copied with their source jobs. Conservative histories are namespaced within a
class-term, use only replayed submissions and share the copy's shift. These copies
are not independently trained predictors or independent workload samples.
% Source: evidence/main_v3/out_main_v31.txt, trace construction table;
% prechecks/weakness1_attack/REPORT.md, development capacity experiment.

For the exact protocol, a copy means the same class-term, replication
round and shift. Its own submission outcomes follow policy-specific completions.
Other classes and terms are exogenous history in that copy's original relative
clock: when the target copy shifts by $s_i$, an external result is interpreted at
$\mathrm{done}_j+s_i$, not at another copy's independent shift. This preserves
earlier completed history without claiming a single platform whose copied users
share dynamically changing state. A separate sensitivity removes entire records
from other replayed classes in the same term, including arrival counts and elapsed
time since arrival; it then repeats same-copy refinement. Ordinary outcome
withholding preserves already-observed arrivals. The event order releases positive
duration completions before reads at the same instant, and zero-duration outcomes
after those reads. These conventions are specified in
\path{docs/policy_consistent_visibility.md} and implemented by
\path{src/spjf_guard/features/refine.py}.

In the package main and visibility tables, each overlay's deadline p99 is first
computed over its eligible jobs; its gap is then computed against that overlay's
FCFS and SJF. Reported p99 and gap are separate arithmetic means over overlays.
Consequently the displayed mean waits need not algebraically recover the displayed
mean gap. Harm and maximum excess are maxima over overlays. Firing is
queue-weighted within a cell, then averaged across overlays. Each bootstrap draw
uses the same week multiplicities for every policy and overlay, recomputes weighted
job quantiles and cell-level ratios, and averages the ratios. No table uses an
average of weekly p99 values. The capacity-sweep gap instead uses the ratio of
averaged overlay quantiles, as explicitly identified in Section~S6.
% Sources: outputs/dev_tables/main_cells.csv and main_table.csv, per-cell/aggregate
% columns; outputs/dev_visibility/visibility_cells.csv and visibility_comparison.csv;
% prechecks/weakness1_attack/capacity_curve.csv. Algorithms: scripts/run_main.py,
% spjf_guard.experiment.metrics and prechecks/weakness1_attack/summarize_capacity.py.

The week-block intervals of the main tables hold the \devnum{40} class-terms fixed and
resample only which weeks the queue sees. Table~\ref{tab:s_cluster} resamples the
class-terms themselves. Each of \devnum{100} draws takes the development class-terms with
replacement, reruns the copy-count probe and the week set on the draw, builds five
overlays with fresh week shifts, and simulates FCFS, SJF, SPJF-E and the three selected
guards at the three loads on the original clock. The predictor and the guard parameters
stay fixed. A draw of the development pool itself reproduces the development tables cell
for cell. The class-term intervals are several times as wide as the week-block ones. At
the busiest load Guard($\devnum{600}$) closes \devnum{0.801} of the gap with interval
\devnum{[0.328, 0.913]}, against \devnum{[0.764, 0.842]} from week blocks, and its paired
difference to unguarded SPJF-E, \devnum{$-0.115$}, has interval \devnum{[-0.320, -0.046]}.
At the two higher loads the difference excludes zero for every $G$. At the lightest load
the difference of Guard($\devnum{1200}$) has interval \devnum{[-0.015, 0.001]}, which
contains zero. With
\devnum{100} draws each end of an interval rests on two or three draws, so the class-term
intervals are coarse.
% Source: outputs/cluster_bootstrap/bootstrap_summary.csv (point, lo, hi, week_lo,

\subsection{Which Results the Package Reproduces}

The package produces the main CodeBench ranking, guard, adversarial and residual
tables from \path{outputs/dev_tables/}, including its separate single-server
directory; predictor metrics from \path{outputs/dev_predictor/}; and visibility
comparisons from \path{outputs/dev_visibility/}. Guard selection and aging selection
are in \path{outputs/selection_v3/} and \path{outputs/selection_aging/}. Their
manifests identify inputs, settings and output hashes. The preserved original
recipe is \path{configs/main_original_84932d9.yaml}; the original outputs are not
claimed to have been regenerated under an unfinished visibility revision.

\begin{sloppypar}
Earlier predictor-family comparisons and source-clock feature sensitivities are
archived under \path{evidence/codebench_service_v2/} and
\path{evidence/predictor_neural/}. The refit-control and GRU rows in the main
ranking table come from \path{evidence/main_v3/}; the ranking-loss table uses its
own fits in \path{evidence/ranking_score/}. Cross-domain rows combine the package
CodeBench row with \path{evidence/cross_domain/}, \path{evidence/accoding_v2/},
\path{evidence/firefox_ci/} and the LPC-EGEE study below. CI chronological-refit
validation is in \path{evidence/firefox_ci_holdout/}. The capacity and physical
experiments are separate analyses in \path{evidence/weakness1_attack/}.
\end{sloppypar}

The trace-construction sensitivity table (Table~\ref{tab:s_sens})
uses the earlier source-clock M4 log-scale score, overlays
\devnum{0}--\devnum{2}, and a fixed completed-work budget
$B=\devnum{600}$ s. This is neither a \devnum{600}-second promise nor the selected
age-relative Guard(\devnum{600}). It supports the earlier ordering result's
sensitivity to term inclusion, the alternate timestamp interpretation, and
merging or dropping co-ending runs. It does not test the final expected-cost
guard, conservative history or policy-specific refinement under those altered
inputs. Numerical agreement of the exact-arithmetic package and its independent
reference is also distinct from the archived earlier-pipeline validation.
% Source: evidence/codebench_service_v2/out_service_v2.txt, SUMMARY 5,
% primary(0-2), SPJF-M4 and GUARD-M4-B600 rows; evidence/main_v3_verify/out_VERDICT.txt.

The sealed procedure is specified by \path{docs/sealed_run_procedure.md}. It
produces separate multiserver, single-server, predictor and visibility outputs,
including the policy-specific exact stage, with unchanged declared selection
rules and parameters. It does not extend the earlier predictor-family or
cross-domain studies. No sealed result is present in this supplement.



\subsection{The Selection Rule and the Cost per Event}
\label{sec:supp_rule}

The selection rule is stated here in full, with the data structures that make the
guard's firing test cost $O(\log n)$ per event, which is the figure Section~\ref*{M-sec:guard} of the
manuscript quotes.

The rule is fixed before the grids are run: three
pre-stated grids, one per shape, \devnum{258} points in all; a point is feasible when
its harm is at most $G/2$ in every one of the \devnum{15} validation cells; the
objective is the deadline-window gap closed in the worst cell, with ties going to
smaller worst harm and then to smaller $B_0$, $\eta$, $\gamma$. Guard($G$) is the
winner over the three grids together; the winner inside each grid is reported as an
ablation row.

We keep
$\mathrm{over}[q] = TC - F(q)$ instead, where $TC$ is the total completed work and
$F(q)$ the prefix sum over ranks up to $q$ of completed work, maintained in a Fenwick
tree, so that a completion is one point update and a query one prefix sum. The firing
test is a segment tree over the window of waiting ranks supporting suffix range-add
and the query ``leftmost leaf with value at least $T$'', after rewriting
$\mathrm{over}[q](t) \ge B_{0,q} + \eta k (t - a_q)$, where $B_{0,q} = B_0 + \gamma n_q$
is fixed at $q$'s arrival, with $\eta k = e_n/e_d$ in lowest terms as
$e_n a_q - e_d B_{0,q} - e_d F(q) \ge e_n t - e_d\, TC$,
whose left side is a per-job value changed only by range-add at completions and whose
right side depends on $t$ alone. The capped channel needs only the head, since at a
fixed threshold $\mathrm{over}$ is non-increasing in rank. All budget arithmetic is
carried in integer microseconds, so the test is exact and no floating-point clock
enters the schedule. Each event costs $O(\log n)$ in the guard's own maintenance,
exclusive of what the base policy spends producing a proposal, and $n$ counts
waiting jobs, with arrivals sharing a timestamp processed in rank order.

\subsection{What the Selection Was Run On}
\label{sec:supp_selection_scope}

What the released package regenerates and what remains archived development evidence are
recorded here, behind the three statements Section~\ref*{M-sec:exp_prov} of the manuscript places before
its numbers.

Third, the grids, the feasibility rule, the objective and the tie-break were fixed before the
grids were run, at comparable density across the three shapes, and the package comparisons use exact integer microseconds for arrivals, service
and budget arithmetic. Earlier predictor comparisons, cross-domain analyses and
trace-construction sensitivities remain archived development evidence, with their
different fits and configurations identified in the captions and this section. They are not all regenerated by the package. Two properties of the outcome belong here. The winning shape is not the same at
every promise: the age-relative
shape wins the joint comparison at $G = \devnum{300}$ and $\devnum{600}$ s and the queue-length
shape at $G = \devnum{1200}$ s, where it reaches worst-cell gap \devnum{0.7275} at worst-cell
harm \devnum{152.2} s against \devnum{482.2} s for the best age-relative point. And a worst-cell
rule can be decided by a single cell: dropping the one validation cell that decides
the $G = \devnum{600}$ choice moves that choice to $B_0 = \devnum{15}k/4$ s, $\eta =
\devnum{0.90}$, with worst-cell gap \devnum{0.7237}. The sealed terms were opened
once after the protocol freeze, with the parameters unchanged, and Section~S10 gives
their tables. The frozen-run procedure also produced a separate single-server trace
and its tables, per-term and pooled predictor metrics, conservative/static visibility
tables and exposure/lag audits, the policy-specific exact-visibility stage and the
online replay. It does not produce the refit-control and GRU rows of
Table~\ref{tab:s_rank}, the other predictor-family comparisons, cross-domain sealed
results, or the earlier trace-construction sensitivities.
% Source of the paragraph above: protocol_lock.draft.json (grids, rule, selected points);
%   docs/repo_build_status.md third pass, sections 1 and 3 (the three grids at comparable
%   density, the nine family winners, the integer-microsecond kernel and the deterministic
%   refit); evidence/main_v3/out_main_v32.txt section 4 (the drop-one-cell sensitivity table,
%   row G = 600 capped) and section 1 (what the second decimal of the older floating-point runs
%   does).


\subsection{The Compared Policies in Full}
\label{sec:supp_policies}

Table~\ref*{M-tab:policies} of the manuscript gives each compared policy a one-line role. What each one is
there for, at length:

\begin{itemize}
\item SJF sorts waiting jobs by their true service times. It is an undeployable
  reference and not an optimum, and it is the denominator of the fraction of the gap
  closed.
\item SPJF-E carries prediction quality through to waiting time, unguarded. SPJF-log
  is the same pipeline fitted on the log scale, and the other candidate scores of
  Section~\ref*{M-sec:rank} of the manuscript run through the same simulator.
\item Guard($G$) takes the budget shape the selection rule of Table~\ref{tab:s_setup}
  picks over all three grids together. Guard-fixed($G$), Guard-age($G$) and
  Guard-queue($G$) take the shape the same rule picks inside the constant,
  age-relative and queue-length grid respectively; they isolate what the shape changes
  at a fixed cap, and one of the three coincides with Guard($G$) at each promise.
\item Fixed($G$) carries the promise $G$ but not the harm constraint, so the selection
  rule rejects it as infeasible. It is reported as the comparator that uses the whole
  cap.
\item Skip($G$) counts positions rather than work, in the finite-skip family, at
  $\kappa = \lfloor Gk/L \rfloor - (2k-2)$, the largest count that still promises $G$
  under dispatch-time charging (Section~S4.4 here).
\item The corrupted scores are a reversed order, a random order and a score that calls
  the truly longest \devnum{1\%} the shortest; they test the guard where the base policy
  is worst. The counterexample traces are retained from two audits and adversarial
  review, and every job in them must satisfy the bounds of Section~\ref*{M-sec:theory} of the manuscript.
\end{itemize}
% Thirteenth pass: the longer role descriptions above stood in the Role column of
%   Table 1 of the manuscript, which keeps one line for each policy.

%=================================================================
\section{Capacity, Physical Queueing and Feedback}
\label{sec:supp_physical}

These development experiments address distinct questions. The capacity sweep changes
the size of the constructed pool while preserving its complete offered stream. The
physical experiment checks an executing timed-work service on a selected window.
Neither identifies a historical queue on CodeBench or a causal deployment effect.
The scores are the archived embedded Tweedie predictions, not the later package
refit, conservative score or exact protocol.

\subsection{Capacity Sensitivity on Complete Traces}

The declared common grid uses every integer $k=\devnum{1},\ldots,\devnum{20}$
on all five development overlays; overlay~\devnum{0} alone continues through
$k=\devnum{76}$. This gives \devnum{156} capacity cells. Each replay starts at the
beginning of its complete trace, keeps queue state across weeks and drains all jobs;
there is no time compression, window reset or thinning. The same source jobs occur
in all overlays, so these are alternative constructions rather than independent
deployments. The grid and comparison family were declared after a pilot on the
first overlay, before the full sweep, not before inspecting the development demand.
% Sources: prechecks/weakness1_attack/summary_numbers.json, capacity ranges;
% prechecks/weakness1_attack/verification.json, complete_cells;
% prechecks/weakness1_attack/REPORT.md, declaration and state-continuity protocol.

Table~\ref{tab:s_capacity} reports the average of the overlay-specific deadline
p99 values, with pointwise intervals. Gap closure in this study is the ratio of
differences of those averaged quantiles. This differs from the package main tables,
which average overlay-specific gap ratios. Paired calendar-week resampling uses
\devnum{2{,}000} common draws, recomputing quantiles from the weighted job
distribution, not averaging weekly quantiles. The declared simultaneous band
covers \devnum{80} absolute FCFS-minus-policy differences: each capacity and each
non-oracle policy. It uses a centred coordinate-standardised maximum statistic.
These intervals resample realised outcomes, not queue trajectories; they omit
source-term selection, predictor fitting, shared source jobs, model mismatch,
feedback and cross-week state dependence. Maxima below are trace maxima, not
population confidence bounds.
% Sources: prechecks/weakness1_attack/summary_numbers.json, simultaneous_band;
% prechecks/weakness1_attack/capacity_curve.csv, p99_deadline_s and gap fields.

The negative cells are material. At $k=\devnum{1}$, FCFS-minus-Guard p99 differences
are \devnum{-208.61}, \devnum{-493.79} and \devnum{-1{,}071.72} s for the
\devnum{300}, \devnum{600} and \devnum{1200} s promises; all simultaneous bands
are wholly negative. At $k=\devnum{2}$ all guard point estimates remain negative.
Positive simultaneous lower bounds occur for SPJF-E at $k=\devnum{2}$--\devnum{12},
Guard(\devnum{300}) and Guard(\devnum{600}) at $k=\devnum{4}$--\devnum{12}, and
Guard(\devnum{1200}) at $k=\devnum{3}$--\devnum{12}. At $k=\devnum{14}$ the guard
point improvements are about \devnum{0.37} s but their simultaneous bands cross
zero; at $k=\devnum{20}$ the point differences are zero. A correct per-job bound
does not imply positive tail benefit across queueing capacities.
% Sources: prechecks/weakness1_attack/capacity_curve.csv, Guard rows,
% absolute_p99_improvement_s, simultaneous_improvement_lo/hi;
% prechecks/weakness1_attack/summary_numbers.json, support_by_policy.

For Guard(\devnum{600}) at $k=\devnum{4}$ the maximum excess is \devnum{515.84} s
and harm is \devnum{339.06} s, although it closes
\devnum{0.8000 [0.7620, 0.8388]} of the reference gap. At $k=\devnum{20}$ that
ratio is undefined because the reference gap is not positive, while maximum excess
and harm remain \devnum{124.38} and \devnum{37.37} s. On overlay~\devnum{0}, FCFS
still has one positive wait at $k=\devnum{75}$; at $k=\devnum{76}$ every policy has
zero wait. The latter is the exact maximum infinite-server occupancy, hence a
zero-wait endpoint for this overlay, not a capacity recommendation for a deployment.
% Sources: prechecks/weakness1_attack/capacity_curve.csv, Guard(600), k=4,20,
% max_excess_s, harm_s, gap_closed and interval; extension_curve.csv, k=75,76;
% prechecks/weakness1_attack/pilot.json and summary_numbers.json, endpoint.

\subsection{An Executing Timed-Work Service}

The physical selection rule chooses the complete calendar-aligned
\devnum{300}-second bin with the greatest offered capped work in development
overlay~\devnum{0}, breaking ties by earliest time. Its \devnum{2{,}151} jobs carry
\devnum{4{,}118.215007} s of requested work, \devnum{6.864} times the
two-worker release-window capacity. Each policy starts empty and drains its own
queue. Inter-arrival spacing, requested costs and scores are retained; workers hold
their slots with timed sleeps. This tests queueing and the control rule, not the
portability of submitted-program execution time. It also discards inherited
full-trace backlog, so its percentile is not the capacity sweep's percentile.
% Source: prechecks/weakness1_attack/physical_numbers.json, input.jobs,
% input.offered_requested_work_s/(input.window_s*input.parameters.workers), protocol.

The nominal payload cap is \devnum{60} s; the measured-service premise
$L=\devnum{61}$ s was fixed before execution. Guard parameters are
$B_0=\devnum{30}$ s, $\eta=\devnum{0.5}$, $\gamma=\devnum{0}$ and
$B_{\max}=\devnum{356}$ s, corresponding to the nominal
\devnum{300}-second ideal-model promise. A measured holding time above $L$ would
fail the premise, not be clipped or used to raise $L$ retrospectively. None did.
Actual enqueue, choice, dispatch, worker start/finish and completion receipt are
recorded separately; service becomes known only at completion receipt.
% Source: prechecks/weakness1_attack/physical_numbers.json, input.parameters.guard;
% prechecks/weakness1_attack/physical_verification.json, service-limit checks.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\caption{Physical timed-work runs. The ideal comparator uses each run's own measured
arrivals and holding costs; all entries are seconds.}
\label{tab:s_physical}
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
\toprule
Policy & Mean wait & p99 wait & Max absolute per-job ideal discrepancy \\
\midrule
FCFS & \devnum{1{,}337.05} & \devnum{1{,}763.30} & \devnum{0.49} \\
% Source: prechecks/weakness1_attack/physical_numbers.json, policies.FCFS, wait_s and absolute_physical_minus_ideal_wait_s.
SPJF-E & \devnum{142.79} & \devnum{1{,}687.47} & \devnum{392.84} \\
% Source: prechecks/weakness1_attack/physical_numbers.json, policies.SPJF-E, wait_s and absolute_physical_minus_ideal_wait_s.
Guard(300) & \devnum{480.39} & \devnum{1{,}783.65} & \devnum{1{,}354.10} \\
% Source: prechecks/weakness1_attack/physical_numbers.json, policies.Guard(300), wait_s and absolute_physical_minus_ideal_wait_s.
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}


All \devnum{6{,}453} observed-prefix decisions passed an independent choice audit.
Physical and ideal measured-input Guard firing counts were \devnum{322} and
\devnum{372}. The small mean discrepancies do not establish job-level fidelity:
the maximum absolute per-job error is \devnum{392.84} s for SPJF-E and
\devnum{1{,}354.10} s for Guard. Guard's physical p99 exceeds the separate physical
FCFS run by \devnum{20.34} s. Thus the physical window confirms an executing
queue and rule conformance but does not show a guarded p99 gain. Actual arrivals
and holding costs differ between physical runs; their comparison is descriptive.
One selected window supplies no across-window confidence interval.
% Sources: prechecks/weakness1_attack/physical_numbers.json, policies.*.audit,
% absolute_physical_minus_ideal_wait_s and wait_s; physical_verification.json,
% Guard(300) ideal forced_count. Difference=1783.64837275-1763.3048828.

The guard's theorem reference is shadow FCFS on that guard run's measured arrivals
and service, not the separately timed physical FCFS run. An observed-trace
certificate augments the ideal bound by cumulative idle worker-time and
dispatch-to-start delay. Its largest correction is \devnum{1.127} s;
all \devnum{2{,}151} jobs pass, and the largest uncorrected excess over shadow FCFS
is \devnum{222.21} s. This is an ex post certificate conditional on the measured
trace and the \devnum{61} s service premise. It does not bound future operating-system
stalls and does not restore an advance uncorrected real-time promise.
% Sources: prechecks/weakness1_attack/stall_bound_check.json, metrics.
% maximum_additive_correction_twice_ns/2e9=1.1265093, corrected_violations=0;
% prechecks/weakness1_attack/physical_verification.json, Guard(300).max_exact_excess_s.

\subsection{Why Open-Loop Benefit Has No Known Bias Direction}

The replay compares policies conditional on fixed offered work. Feitelson states the
objection at its strongest, that ``logged workloads actually contain a `signature' of the
logged system'' \cite{feitelson2021resampling}: a trace-driven simulation issues requests
irrespective of system state \cite{shmueli2009simulation}, so faster replies cannot
induce more submissions, change their costs or alter which jobs enter a deadline-window
quantile. Shmueli and Feitelson also distinguish increased productivity from improvement
in conventional waiting metrics \cite{shmueli2009simulation}. The two constructions below
show that the bias from the missing feedback has no known direction.
% Ninth pass: the framing above stood in Section 9 of the manuscript, which keeps the
%   statement that the results are conditional on fixed offered work.

In an exact
single-server feedback construction, jobs of length \devnum{10} and \devnum{1} s
arrive together, the long job first, and the short job's user submits a second
unit job \devnum{0.5} s after completion. Replaying the FCFS-induced arrivals
gives SJF a mean-wait advantage of \devnum{3.00} s; letting each policy generate
its own successor arrival changes the advantage to \devnum{-0.167} s.
The corresponding linearly interpolated p99 advantages are \devnum{8.82} and
\devnum{0.47} s. The job cohort is identical, so this example does not depend on
comparing different populations.
% Source: prechecks/weakness1_attack/out_feedback_counterexamples.txt, EXAMPLE 1,
% exact Fraction schedules and open_loop/closed_loop mean and linear_p99 gains.

The opposite bias direction is possible too. An initial FCFS order with services
\devnum{20}, \devnum{1}, \devnum{3} and \devnum{10} s, whose unit-job user returns
after \devnum{2} s with a \devnum{0.1} s successor, gives open- and closed-loop
mean advantages of \devnum{9.20} and \devnum{11.16} s and p99 advantages of
\devnum{10.00} and \devnum{10.18} s. These exact recursions falsify a general
lower-bound interpretation; they are not fitted behavioural models. The pathwise
theorem still compares a guarded schedule with shadow FCFS on that schedule's
realised arrivals and services, not independently evolving closed-loop systems
\cite{shmueli2009simulation}.
% Source: prechecks/weakness1_attack/out_feedback_counterexamples.txt, EXAMPLE 2,
% parameters, exact schedules and gains; final assertion status PASS.

\subsection{LPC-EGEE: Recorded Queueing with Weak Counterfactual Validation}
\label{sec:supp_lpc}

The public LPC-EGEE trace supplies recorded waits and concentrated execution costs
together \cite{lpc_egee_trace}. After outage exclusion and queue-limit capping,
the longest \devnum{1\%} of \devnum{206{,}222} retained jobs carries
\devnum{40.7\%} of work. On the chronological evaluation part the top share is
\devnum{35.1\%}, which is the value used in the main cross-domain table.
The physical pools stay disjoint. Effective capacities \devnum{83} and
\devnum{56} are fitted only on the earlier part, with no test-driven refit.
Service is the minimum of recorded run time and its documented queue limit;
the common bound uses the largest pool limit, \devnum{259{,}200} s.
% Sources: prechecks/lpc_egee/applicability.csv, all/test aggregate rows;
% prechecks/lpc_egee/capacity.json and policy_parameters.csv.

The fitted FCFS approximation underestimates evaluation-part mean waits by
\devnum{24\%} and \devnum{39\%}, with hourly correlations of \devnum{0.44} and
\devnum{0.40}. The archive describes plain FCFS serial-job ordering during the
logged period, but queue concurrency ceilings, polling and middleware are absent
from the work-conserving replay. Recorded waits therefore cannot be equated with
the theoretical FCFS counterfactual. This is weak simulator validation, not
evidence that the original scheduler was an arbitrary non-FCFS policy.
% Source: prechecks/lpc_egee/validation.csv, fitted/test rows: mean ratios
% 0.757708/0.614029 and hourly Pearson 0.439849/0.397983; archive documentation.

In the conditional replay, SPJF-E reduces mean wait by
\devnum{21.4\% [13.2\%, 38.2\%]}, but its busy-hour p99 reduction of
\devnum{4.2\% [-11.2\%, 46.0\%]} remains unresolved. Busy hours use frozen
arrival-count thresholds, not deadlines. All six constant/age work-budget guards
at the tested multiples of $L$ fire zero times; a small fraction of the numerical
bound being spent is therefore not evidence of effective protection. Histories
use recorded completion times, and \devnum{2{,}906} evaluation arrivals
(\devnum{2.5\%}) have an outcome unavailable under replayed SPJF-E. This row is a
recorded-feature conditional counterfactual, not a closed-loop deployment result.
% Sources: prechecks/lpc_egee/pooled_policy_metrics.csv, SPJF-E mean and busy-p99
% reduction/interval fields; guard_dispatches.csv, all Constant/Age forced_sum=0;
% history_counterfactual_diagnostic.csv, affected count/share.


\subsection{Closed-Loop Replay with the Recorded Think Times}
\label{sec:supp_closed_loop}

Section~S6.3 leaves the direction of the missing feedback unidentified. On the main
judging trace one component of that feedback is recorded, and this subsection replays the
development overlays a second time with it.

The logs identify each submission's author and its position in that student's sequence,
so for two consecutive submissions of the same student-term the offset
$\delta_i = a_i - (a_{\mathrm{pred}} + C_{\mathrm{pred}})$ between the previous result and
the next submission is in the data. It is non-negative for \devnum{98.38\%} of the
\devnum{17{,}566{,}120} consecutive pairs, and for \devnum{97.17\%} of the
\devnum{4{,}217{,}092} pairs inside deadline windows, so on the header reading of
Section~\ref*{M-sec:data} of the manuscript the next submission almost always follows the previous
result. Its median is \devnum{123.5} s over all pairs and \devnum{109.0} s inside
windows, with \devnum{11.7} s at the tenth percentile and \devnum{35.0} s at the
twenty-fifth, and it carries no measurable dependence on the previous job's predicted
cost: the Spearman correlation is \devnum{0.015} over \devnum{2{,}000{,}000} sampled
result-gated pairs. The chains are \devnum{68{,}640} in number, \devnum{1{,}560} real
student-terms replicated \devnum{44} times across \devnum{17{,}634{,}760} jobs.
% Source: prechecks/closed_loop/out/think_time.json, rep0: share_result_gated;
% delta_in_window.share_result_gated and delta_in_window.n_with_predecessor;
% delta_all_s q0.1, q0.25, q0.5; delta_in_window.quantiles_s q0.5;
% spearman_delta_vs_c_pred_result_gated and n_sampled_for_correlation; n_jobs,
% n_first_submissions and n_with_predecessor.

The closed rule releases a submission at its predecessor's completion in the replay plus
that pair's recorded $\delta_i$ when $\delta_i \ge 0$, and preserves the recorded
inter-submission gap otherwise; the open rule is the recorded instants the rest of the
paper uses. With gating switched off, the closed kernel reproduces the package simulator
job for job, at a maximum absolute wait difference of \devnum{0} microseconds over four
policies at three load levels, and its open-loop rows reproduce the main development
cells to every printed digit.
% Source: prechecks/closed_loop/out/validation.json, max_abs_diff_us for FCFS, SJF,
% SPJF-E and Guard(600) at level0/level1/level2; prechecks/closed_loop/out/closed_cells.csv,
% mode=open rows against outputs/dev_tables/main_cells.csv.

% Source of Table~\ref{tab:s_closed}: prechecks/closed_loop/out/open_vs_closed_mean.csv,
% columns p99_dl_s and gap_closed at mode=open, mode=closed/late_submissions=kept and

The ordering of the policies survives in all nine cells and under both edge treatments,
and the scale does not: the deadline-window p99 falls by a factor of \devnum{1.3} to
\devnum{3.8} and the mean wait by \devnum{1.5} to \devnum{3.9}. A
queue that builds up holds back the submissions that would have deepened it, so the
realised busiest-hour utilisation under FCFS drops from \devnum{0.505}, \devnum{0.807}
and \devnum{1.009} to \devnum{0.450}, \devnum{0.745} and \devnum{0.942}, and the heaviest
level is no longer overloaded. The baseline gains most from this, because its own longer
waits delay its users more: at the heaviest level the mean arrival drift is
\devnum{440.0} s under FCFS against \devnum{171.5} s under SPJF-E and \devnum{142.3} s
under SJF. The absolute gap therefore narrows while the fraction of it a policy closes
does not, which is why the closed columns of Table~\ref{tab:s_closed} move in both
directions and stay inside \devnum{0.81}--\devnum{0.85}.
% Source: prechecks/closed_loop/out/open_vs_closed_mean.csv, rho_busy_hour at
% mode=open and mode=closed and drift_mean_s, FCFS/SPJF-E/SJF rows at each level,
% late_submissions=kept.

What changes qualitatively is the price of the promise, not its value. Gating shortens
the queues, so the guard fires far less often: the queue-weighted firing rate of
Guard(\devnum{600}) falls from \devnum{0.113\%} to \devnum{0.0025\%} at the lightest
level, from \devnum{1.314\%} to \devnum{0.134\%} at the middle one and from
\devnum{7.521\%} to \devnum{0.272\%} at the heaviest. Its deadline-window p99 penalty
over unguarded SPJF-E falls with it, from \devnum{41.6\%} to \devnum{3.7\%} at the
heaviest level and from \devnum{9.6\%} to \devnum{1.3\%} at the middle one. The worst
wait it admits is unchanged in kind: \devnum{1{,}074.3} s under gating, where SPJF-E
reaches \devnum{5{,}783.2} s and SJF \devnum{4{,}234.3} s, so starvation of long jobs
remains a property of the ranking and not of the arrival process. The per-job guarantee
is unaffected. It is checked against FCFS run open-loop on the guarded policy's own
realised arrival sequence, which is the counterfactual Section~\ref*{M-sec:model} of the manuscript
defines, and it holds on every job of every run: zero violations in \devnum{18} guarded
runs of \devnum{17{,}634{,}760} jobs each, with the worst job using \devnum{80} to
\devnum{90}~\% of its bound under gating against \devnum{87} to \devnum{95}~\% without
it. Refusing the submissions that gating pushes past their own assignment's end removes
\devnum{0.27} to \devnum{1.93}~\% of the offered work and moves the p99 by at most
\devnum{1.3}~\%.
% Source: prechecks/closed_loop/out/open_vs_closed_mean.csv,
% fired_fraction_queue_weighted, p99_dl_s, max_s, bound_violations,
% worst_ratio_to_bound, n_dropped and offered_work_s; Guard(600), SPJF-E and SJF rows
% at each level and mode.

A paired week-block bootstrap on overlay \devnum{0}, \devnum{400} resamples of whole
weeks with replacement, puts the closed-minus-open difference in the gap closed at

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
$\rho$ & SPJF-E & Guard(\devnum{600}) \\
\midrule
\devnum{0.5} & \devnum{$+0.092$} [\devnum{0.000}, \devnum{0.174}] & \devnum{$+0.123$} [\devnum{0.043}, \devnum{0.193}] \\
\devnum{0.8} & \devnum{$-0.071$} [\devnum{$-0.108$}, \devnum{$-0.025$}] & \devnum{$-0.025$} [\devnum{$-0.077$}, \devnum{0.036}] \\
\devnum{1.0} & \devnum{$-0.055$} [\devnum{$-0.089$}, \devnum{$-0.007$}] & \devnum{$+0.010$} [\devnum{$-0.037$}, \devnum{0.063}] \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
% Source: prechecks/closed_loop/out/bootstrap.json, paired closed-minus-open
% difference in gap_closed, mean and 2.5/97.5 percentiles, overlay 0, 400 resamples,
% levels 0-2, SPJF-E and Guard(600). The table is written by hand from that file
% (prechecks/closed_loop/REPORT.md:163-174 printed +0.093 for 0.09246; corrected here).

\noindent so the movement is resolved in four of the six comparisons and is small in
every one. This is a reduction of the scheme Section~S5.3 describes, which uses
\devnum{2{,}000} resamples over five overlays, and it is a cost decision rather than a
methodological one.

Four limits stand. The think times were recorded on a platform that gave each student a
private container, so every $\delta$ in the trace was produced by a student who never
waited, and the replay assumes $\delta$ is invariant to the wait; nothing in this trace
separates impatience from disengagement. The model has no abandonment and no content
feedback, so it does not address the productivity distinction Shmueli and Feitelson draw
\cite{shmueli2009simulation}, which Section~S6.3 states. The chains are \devnum{44}
copies of \devnum{1{,}560} real student-terms, so the effective sample for anything
driven by user behaviour is \devnum{1{,}560} and the week-block bootstrap, which
resamples time, does not cover that. And the comparison stops being pathwise: each
policy generates its own arrival sequence, so the closed columns compare different sample
paths over the same job population, although the per-job theorem, stated against FCFS on
the realised sequence, still applies job by job.

\subsection{LPC-EGEE per Walltime Class: A Limit Class Where the Guard Fires}
\label{sec:supp_lpc_queues}

Section~S6.4 pools the six documented walltime classes of the LPC-EGEE log and uses the
largest limit, \devnum{259{,}200} s, as $L$. Reading each class as its own pool, with
that class's own limit, changes the promise by two and a half orders of magnitude: on the
\devnum{900}-second class the work-budget guard fires and comes within \devnum{0.7\%} of
its bound, where the pooled reading of the same log never fires and spends
\devnum{0.046} of it. It does not make the first applicability condition robust on this
trace.
% Source: prechecks/lpc_egee_queues/bound_checks.csv, queue_name=test
% policy=Constant-3.5L, worst_ratio = 0.9933; prechecks/lpc_egee/bound_checks.csv,
% Constant-3.5L worst_ratio = 0.0464, the pooled reading whose guards never fire.

The class pool is a construction of ours, and measurably so. The SWF header names three
machines, not the two the pooled reading used: separating \texttt{clrglop195}, which owns
no held-out arrival, leaves CE1 with \devnum{111{,}236} jobs and CE2 with
\devnum{60{,}519}, and CE2 alone then maxes out at exactly \devnum{56} simultaneous jobs,
which is its documented processor count and resolves the earlier audit's unexplained
\devnum{58}. Within each partition the per-class maxima of simultaneously running jobs
sum to \devnum{337} against \devnum{84} processors on CE1 and to \devnum{203} against
\devnum{56} on CE2, so the classes demonstrably shared their partition's machines and a
per-class pool is a constructed pool on the same footing as the others in this paper.

% n_test) and "Queues shared the machines of their partition" (nominal_cpus,
% sum_of_queue_maxima).

The \devnum{900}-second \texttt{test} class is the one cell that behaves differently. Its
recorded p99 queue wait is \devnum{23.885} times its own limit over the whole log, and
\devnum{7.573} and \devnum{18.675} times on CE1 and CE2 over the held-out part; no other
class approaches it, and pooled over all six the same ratio is \devnum{0.046}. Cost
concentration inside a capped class is correspondingly weaker than pooled: the longest
\devnum{1\%} of the class carry \devnum{0.213} of its held-out executed work,
\devnum{0.167} on CE1 and \devnum{0.350} on CE2, against \devnum{0.407} when the six
classes are pooled, so part of the heavy tail of a mixed pool is the mixing of limits
itself. The predictor, refitted per class on the earlier part with the package defaults,
reaches a held-out AUROC of \devnum{0.746} on the \texttt{test} class, \devnum{0.846} on
\texttt{short}, \devnum{0.792} on \texttt{infinite} and \devnum{0.737} on \texttt{long}.

% prechecks/lpc_egee_queues/policy_metrics.csv, scope=test queue_name=test: mean,

% max_excess; firing_rate from guard_dispatches.csv, queue_name=test. The same rows are
% rendered in prechecks/lpc_egee_queues/tables.md, sections "Replay, test queue
% (L = 900 s)" and "Guard firing and per-job bound checks".

The guard fires at \devnum{1.42\%} of dispatch epochs at the tightest promise and its
per-job bound is nearly attained: the largest realised excess over the FCFS wait is
\devnum{3{,}129} s against an allowance of \devnum{3{,}150} s, a ratio of
\devnum{0.9933}, where the pooled reading of Section~S6.4 reached \devnum{0.046}. Over
\devnum{1{,}021{,}494} job-policy checks there is no violation. The trade-off is visible
in one line: the guard gives up \devnum{0.11} of the mean gap the unguarded score closes
and cuts the worst excess from \devnum{77{,}467} s to \devnum{3{,}129} s, with harm of
\devnum{25} s under both. The busy-hour p99 is not improved by any guarded policy, every
interval spanning zero.
% Source: prechecks/lpc_egee_queues/bound_checks.csv, queue_name=test
% policy=Constant-3.5L: max_excess_s, theoretical_max_s, worst_ratio and violations;
% guard_dispatches.csv, same rows, n_forced over n_dispatch summed across the partitions
% (585 of 41,143 = 1.42 %); policy_metrics.csv scope=test, mean_gap for SPJF-E and
% Constant-3.5L and harm; prechecks/lpc_egee_queues/REPORT.md section 7 for the total
% check count. Both are rendered in tables.md, section "Guard firing and per-job bound
% checks".

Four things keep this from being evidence of deployment benefit. The capacity that makes
the first condition pass is the boundary value $k=\devnum{1}$ of the search grid, chosen
on \devnum{7.6} days of pre-cutoff data on CE1 and \devnum{14.2} on CE2, and at the
class's own observed concurrency ceiling the held-out FCFS p99 is exactly zero on both
partitions, so the condition fails there; on CE2 it also fails the rule-of-thumb
\devnum{3}$L$ form at the fitted capacity, the FCFS p99 being \devnum{2.448}$L$. The
held-out wait profile is not reproduced: the per-class simulator is \devnum{2.9} times
high on CE1 and \devnum{7.3} times low on CE2 on the mean, with hourly correlations
indistinguishable from zero, and for the \devnum{7{,}200}-second class it produces
identically zero waits against a recorded p99 of \devnum{14{,}288.96} s. The mechanism is
the one the concurrency sums show: a short-limit job's recorded wait at this site is
largely time spent behind other classes' long jobs on shared machines, which a per-class
pool deletes by construction. And the search that found this cell covered eleven cells,
two capacity readings, three promises and two budget shapes, which the intervals do not
cover.

% profile, primary variant" (recorded mean, sim mean and hourly r for CE1 test, CE2 test
% and CE1 short) and section "The same, at the observed concurrency ceiling" (fcfs_p99 =
% 0); section "Applicability per cell, held-out part", CE2 test fcfs_p99_over_L = 2.45;
% prechecks/lpc_egee_queues/REPORT.md sections 4 and 9 for the fit-window lengths and
% the multiplicity count.

\subsection{Footnotes to the Cross-Trace Table}
\label{sec:supp_crossnotes}

Table~\ref*{M-tab:cross} of the manuscript points here for the fitted capacities of its two
continuous-integration rows and for the LPC-EGEE grid log \cite{lpc_egee_trace}, whose two
rows Table~\ref{tab:s_lpc_rows} gives in the same format. LPC-EGEE records both waits and
concentrated costs, but its fitted FCFS approximation underestimates the mean waits of the
chronological test, and the one reading on which a guard fires is a per-class pool we
impose ourselves. Both rows are therefore conditional counterfactuals and not estimates of
deployment benefit; Sections~\ref{sec:supp_lpc} and~\ref{sec:supp_lpc_queues} give the two
runs. Each paragraph below says what a row does and does not support.

\paragraph{The two continuous-integration capacities.}
The CI capacities are fitted, not recorded, and the two scheduling columns and the FCFS
p99 of those rows exist only at the whole-window fit ($k_{\mathrm{eff}} = \devnum{156}$
of \devnum{173} and \devnum{96} of \devnum{105}), so they are printed at it. What
supports them is the held-out refit of Section~\ref*{M-sec:source_platform} of the manuscript, which recovers the
same capacity to within a few machines on chronological splits of each window and
reproduces the recorded waits on the untouched part, with the p90 the one quantile that
does not survive. The tail share and the AUROC do not depend on the capacity.

\paragraph{The LPC-EGEE chronological test.}
LPC-EGEE \cite{lpc_egee_trace} uses disjoint pools with \devnum{83} and \devnum{56}
servers fitted on the earlier part, and full chronological test wait-profile validation
is weak. $L$ is the largest documented queue limit. The p99 and the gaps of that row use
frozen-threshold busy hours, not deadlines, and history follows recorded completions.
The displayed age-relative guard at $G=\devnum{5}L$ never fires. This conditional FCFS
counterfactual is not evidence of real-scheduler improvement; Section~S6.4 here gives
the run.
% Sources: prechecks/lpc_egee/capacity.json, policy_parameters.csv and
% guard_dispatches.csv, Age-5L, forced_sum=0; pooled_policy_metrics.csv.

\paragraph{The imposed \texttt{test}-class pool.}
The \texttt{test} class is a pool we impose on machines the six queues demonstrably
shared, at the boundary capacity $k=\devnum{1}$ of the search grid on each partition,
and its held-out wait profile is not reproduced. Condition~1 holds on both partitions'
recorded waits and on the pooled FCFS counterfactual, and fails at the \devnum{3}$L$
form on one partition and at the observed concurrency ceiling on both. The
\devnum{0.679} is the unguarded score's busy-hour p99 gap, whose interval spans zero,
and the guard's own is \devnum{$-0.103$}, so the last column of that row reports its
resolved mean gap, \devnum{0.569} [\devnum{0.534}, \devnum{0.630}], at
$G = \devnum{3.5}L$ rather than the \devnum{5}$L$ of the other rows, where its largest
excess is \devnum{3{,}129} s of an allowed \devnum{3{,}150} s
(Section~\ref{sec:supp_lpc_queues}).
% Sources: prechecks/lpc_egee_queues/tables.md, "Queues shared the machines of their
% partition" (nominal_cpus, sum_of_queue_maxima) and "Applicability per cell, held-out
% part" (CE2 test fcfs_p99_over_L = 2.448, obs rows fcfs_p99 = 0); REPORT.md section 4
% for the 7.6 and 14.2 fit-window days; policy_metrics.csv scope=test, p99_busy_gap and
% mean_gap with its interval; bound_checks.csv, queue_name=test Constant-3.5L
% max_excess_s and theoretical_max_s.
% Twelfth pass: the three paragraphs above were footnotes c, d and e of Table 7 of the
%   manuscript, which keeps the markers on the cells and one line pointing here.

\subsection{What Failing an Applicability Condition Does Not Establish}
\label{sec:supp_applicability}

Section~\ref*{M-sec:limits} of the manuscript names two applicability conditions and the traces that
fail them, and Section~\ref*{M-sec:exp_cross} there gives the numbers trace by trace. What a failure
does and does not support is here.

Under the first condition, the four failing workloads have a first-come first-served
p99 wait between \devnum{0.021} and \devnum{1.78} multiples of $L$, against the
additive term of about \devnum{3}$L$ in the certificate. The Intel Netbatch compute
farm \cite{netbatch2012trace,shai2014netbatch} is the extreme. Its service cap is
\devnum{5.7} days against a worst-window p99 wait of \devnum{18.7} hours, so the
uniform additive term is about \devnum{17} days, the guard never fires at any
positive budget tested, and predicted-cost ordering makes the p99 wait worse than
first-come first-served there under sustained overload. That does not establish
impossibility below the term for a restricted base policy or workload, and the guard
results the manuscript reports on those four workloads measure the ordering mechanism
there.

The second condition is an empirical opportunity screen. It is not a theorem
assumption and not a necessary condition for a benefit. On the two Firefox CI
hardware pools \cite{taskcluster_docs,treeherder_docs} the longest \devnum{1\%} of
jobs carry \devnum{3.4\%} and \devnum{5.2\%} of the work, at coefficients of
variation \devnum{0.61} and \devnum{0.87}, and there even true-size ordering makes
the p99 wait worse than first-come first-served on three of the four windows we ran.
% Thirteenth pass: the two paragraphs above stood in Section 9 of the manuscript,
%   which keeps three sentences and this pointer.

\subsection{The Guard on a Course Platform's Own Two-Worker Back End}
\label{sec:supp_platform}

The timed-work experiment of Section~S6.2 ran the guard on a service whose jobs were
sleeps of known length. This run puts it on a real one. The service is the back end of
an online course platform whose jobs are calls to a language model: a short call that
writes a learner report and a long one that writes a study plan. Two worker slots serve
one queue. The server stops a job at an enforced limit, \devnum{25} s for a report and
\devnum{170} s for a plan; a stopped job holds its slot until the model call has
returned, so the executed work of a stopped job is the limit itself and not a
number read off afterwards. Dispatch, the guard and the limit were added to the
platform's own code and run locally on the authors' machine; nothing was deployed,
and no human learner was in the loop. The demand came from \devnum{20} scripted
accounts. Each account submitted in the class pattern of the platform's own users,
six reports for every two plans, in a burst shaped like the deadline hours of the
main trace, and waited for each result before submitting again; the think time was
drawn per seat from one seed, so it was the same in every cell. The promise was
$G=\devnum{400}$ s with $k=\devnum{2}$, budget shape $B_0=\devnum{30}$ s and
$\eta=\devnum{0.5}$, the shape of the timed-work experiment and not a new selection.
Each cell ran \devnum{100} jobs.

simulator fed the same inputs; the reservation cell is not comparable because the
simulator has no reservation-and-refund wrapper.}
\label{tab:s_platform}
